OLD COURT.
FIFTH DAY. TUESDAY, DECEMBER 9.
Third Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
207. WILLIAM LEWIS was indicted for feloniously assaulting George Ball, on the King's highway, on the 19th of October, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 hat, value 5s.; 6 half-crowns, and 1 shilling, his property.
GEORGE BALL. I am a journeyman cabinet-maker, and live in Matilda-street, Hackney-road. On the 19th of October, about one o'clock in the morning, I was at the top of Brick-lane, Bethnal-green, going home - I was quite sober; four or five men came upon me - they surrounded me, beat me violently with their fists, and bruised me; I was knocked down, and my hat taken off my head, - I lost six half-crowns and a shilling from my pocket- I am certain I did not drop it; the prisoner might be there, but I did not see him in that attack; they went away - I went on, and about a minute's walk from there the prisoner came up with my hat in his hand - it might be about twenty yards from where I was robbed, but not more, and not three minutes had elapsed; I had got up about two minutes - the prisoner came across the road, and struck me three blows with his fist; I fell from the last blow - he was an entire stranger to me; he had a hat on, and another in his hand, which was mine; then the other party, who had robbed me, rushed violently on me again, and kicked me - there were four of them on me; they beat me so that I was not able to get up - I was takenby four men on their shoulders to the watch-house; the watchmen and officers found me lying on the ground - I had lain there about three minutes; I am sure the prisoner struck me, and after I was down the party, who robbed me, and after I was down the party, who robbed me, struck - my head and ears were cut; I was badly wounded - I am sure the men who came up were the same as had robbed me; I was not capable of giving charge of the prisoner that night from my blows and wounds; the officers came down to me next morning, and asked if I meant to charge him with robbery or the assault; I said the robbery - he was taken on the spot.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you not know that he was robbed himself? A. No; I heard somebody had run away with his change, from the bar of a public-house - he said something of that sort at the watch-house; I cannot tell now whether he did say so or not -I believe he is deaf; I talked to him loud enough to make him hear.
Q. You did not ask him for 2l. to let him go? A. No; it was his first proposition to me; I do not know Jones, the watchman - Stokes, the beadle, was present, and said he would not have such a thing arranged there; I said if his master could raise 2l., but it was not my first proposition - I have not had 1s.
COURT. Q.Explain this? A.When I went to the watch-house, he fell on his knees, and begged me not to prosecute him, and said he would give ever so much to be liberated; I then said, "Can you raise 2l.?" he said he could raise 1l.; I said could not his master raise the other pound.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You would let him go for 2l. though you were sure he had given you the beating you have described? A. Yes; he did not appear stupified, for he was very resolute with the watchman.
BENJAMIN NORRIS. I am a watchman. I was at the corner of Gibraltar-walk; I had just called one o'clock; I got into my box, with my back to the road - I was reaching my pipe down, and all at once there was a rush behind me; I turned round, and saw the prosecutor lying on the ground, and three men beating him cruelly - there were three men on him; I said, "Halloo! are you going to kill the man?" one of them answered, "You b-g-r you, if you come out we will serve you ten times worse:" I said, "So you shall;" I came out directly, sprung my rattle for assistance, and as soon as I came out they started across the road; they ran three or four doors down James-street, - the prisoner spoke a word or two to the other three, but what it was I cannot say; he returned, met me, and stopped me from pursuing them - he held up his hand, and asked me what I meant; I stepped back, and moved my stick to keep him off, for I expected to receive a blow every moment; two officers, hearing my rattle, came to my assistance; I took him with his hands out at me, asking me what I meant - he was then about ten yards from where the prosecutor lay.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you not know his hand was only up to his ear, to assist himself in hearing what was said? A.His hand was up near his ear, but his fist was doubled - he could hear me without my going near him; I was afraid to go near for fear of a blow; nobody spoke to him while I was in the watch-house - I never saw him with more than one hat; he had no stick.
JAMES GLIBBERY. I am a constable. I was in Brick-lane, between one and two o'clock on Sunday morning, the 19th of October, and heard a rattle sprung; I went up and heard a man lie groaning on the pavement - I found the prisoner with his hand up, as if he was going to strike some one; I laid hold of him, and took him in charge - he certainly seemed in a mad kind of manner, not right in his mind; he fell on his knees, begging him to let him go - he said he was an innocent man, and never did a robbery - I did not then know there had been a robbery.
Cross-examined. Q. He appeared wild? A. Yes; he was rather the worse for drink, I think - he has been out of his mind I am told; I heard something of his having been in a public-house, and somebody running away with his change, whether it was from him I cannot say - it was next day, I think; I know, it was not that night.
MILES HALL. I was with Glibbery. I am a constable, and was present when the prisoner was apprehended; he was near Norris, but I was at a distance, and did not see his hand up; the prosecutor was not well enough to give the charge - we laid him all along in the watch-house, and the blood came from him; we could get nothing from him for some time, and we left him there - he got up about half an hour after; the watch-house-keeper got his direction, and he was led home - I saw him next day, and he described the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you look at the prisoner's hands to see if there were any marks of blood on them? A. No; he had no weapon - we did not know whether there had been a robbery till the morning; I have said I did not think the prisoner was one of them - that was only my idea, because he fell on his knees and prayed, and said he was innocent; he had only one hat - he seemed much alarmed.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent; I came up Church-street, and there was not a soul there; when I came up to the watchman, he said, "Keep off:" - "Well," said I, "my friend, I want to keep off," and they took me to the watch-house, and found 1s. 9d. or 1s. 10d. on me; when the man came to me, he said, "You are the very man who struck me. I bended my knees down before my Maker; and am as innocent as ever there was a prisoner before the bar; I never put a hand on the man - he has certainly sworn my life away; he said I knew where his hat was - I said,"My good friend, there was not a soul with me when I went up to the watchman, if it was the last word I had to say:" he said he could swear to me by the corner of my handkerchief, and said,"Is not that the handkerchief you had on last night;" I said it was; he came to me on the Monday, and said "You are the very man who struck me; if I was to go to your master, and ask him for 2l., don't you think he would lend it you?" I never put a hand on him - I told him I would have my trial and justice.
THOMAS JONES. I am a watchman of Bethnal-green. I was at the watch-house when the prisoner was there - the prosecutor was talking to him at the cage-door; I heard him say if he would give him 2l. he would make it up when he came to Worship-street; I was obliged to go to the watch-house, and did not hear the prisoner's answer - there was only me, Stokes, and the prosecutor there.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-208]
Before Mr. Baron Hullock.
208. RICHARD CATTLE and GEORGE SMITH were indicted for feloniously assaulting Mary Ann Powers, on the 1st of November, putting her in fear, and taking from her person, and against her will, 5 sovereigns, the monies of Elizabeth Smith, widow .
MESSRS. BRODRICK & PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
MARY ANN POWERS. I am servant to Mrs. Elizabeth Smith, of Margaret-street, Hackney; I know the prisoner- Cattle is a baker and pastry-cook, and Smith, I believe, is a baker. On the 1st of November, I went to take a pie to Cattle's shop - Smith came in just as I was coming out: in the course of the time, Smith sent me out for some gin - Cattle poured out about half a glass; I took a little of it, and in the course of conversation, I said I was going to Hoxton, in the evening; both the prisoners were present when I said that; about twenty minutes before six o'clock, I went out from mistress to go to Mr. Hunter's, Britannia-gardens, Hoxton, to receive 5l.; I had a receipt for it in my hand; mistress desired me to go a public way: I passed Cattle's shop in my way - I owed him 1d. for a tart - I went in and paid for it; Smith came in directly I got into the shop - I do not know whether I then mentioned where I was going; the prisoners went out of the shop; I remained there five or ten minutes talking to Mrs. Cattle, and then went out; Cattle overtook me when I got to the Goldsmiths' alms-houses, in Hackney-road, and said he was going my way; I said "Are you?" I saw Smith, in a few minutes he came up; they were talking together - I could not hear what they said; after going on a little while, I heard Cattle tell Smith, what a pretty sweetheart he was, for he had never spoken to me all the way; I went on, and got at last to Pitfield-street, and at the corner of that street, I asked Cattle if he would be so kind as to come down Britannia-gardens, as it was such a lonesome place; he said he was in a hurry to get his yeast, but if I would go as far as St. Luke's church with him, he would see me to Britannia-gardens; I went with him - Smith went also; and when we got a little distance, they both asked me to go into a public-house to have something to drink; I went in, and Smith gave me a glass of gin, which I drank - I stopped by St. Luke's church, while he got his yeast - we were all three together.
Q. Well, after that you set off to Britannia-gardens? A. Yes; I went to Mr. Hunter's, and received five sovereigns - the prisoners waited for me by agreement, till I came out- I wrapped the five sovereigns in a piece of paper, and put them in my bosom - my gown was pinned over it, and my apron tied over that - I found them waiting for me; Cattle said, had I got what I went for; I said Yes - I had the receipt in my hand in his shop; it had a stamp on it; I kept it in my hand all along the road; after going a little way, Cattle said if I had got any paper, or any thing to take care of, he would take care of it for me; I said I was capable of taking care of it myself; they asked me to go to a house, No. 9, opposite Britannia-gardens - I refused; Smith said it would not hurt me - he took me by the hand, and pulled me in: I do not remember whether Cattle said any thing - we all three went in; some gin was called for; Smith brought me a glass to the window, which I drank - I do not know who paid for it; I was standing by the window, and had not sat down; the prisoners were at the bar; I came out with them; we turned down a court by the side of the public-house - it was dark; I said I did not know where I was; Cattle said I was going right; about five or ten minutes after this, I felt very sick and giddy; Cattle repeated two or three times that he would take care of what I had, if I would give it to him; I refused - I do not remember going to any other house, except one, where I had a glass of liquor thrown over me, and I believe that was the Red Cow public-house, at Dalston; Smith threw it over me, because I would not drink it - I have no recollection whatever of what happened after I got out of this public-house, till I remember the patrol taking me up off the ground - I am seventeen years old, and single - I have lived at Mrs. Smith's four years.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q.How far from Cattle's shop does your mistress live? A. A very short distance - about as far as from here to Newgate; I knew him perfectly well - mistress never went to his shop; he was known in the neighbourhood; I was at this house twice that day, but did not say what I was going to Hoxton for - the recipt was folded up in my hand - nobody could see what it was - I do not recollect telling either of them I was going for money; I had only known Smith about three weeks - I had seen him at Cattle's,
Q. As you were insensible from the time you left the public-house till the patrol came up, can you venture to say you did not give Cattle the money to take care of? A. I cannot swear whether I gave it him - whether I dropped it, or whether it was taken from me; I have not the least recollection of giving it to him, and think if I had done so I must recollect it; I wore a close dress, pinned up at the bosom; I put the money down near the pit of my stomach, then put up my dress, and tied my apron over it; the gin thrown over me went down my neck; I did not open my bosom to wipe it.
Q. After you got out, was there no romping or toying between Smith and you? A. Not that I recollect; Cattle's brother came to our house on Sunday morning, while I was at chapel, and I went to where cattle was, but did not see him; I spoke to him throught the rails.
Q. Did you not say to him, "I won't hurt you - I am sure you did not intend to rob me"? A. No, I did not; he said "If you say I took the money from you, it will transport me for life," and I said "I cannot say whether I gave it you or not;" that is all I said.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Why did you go to the watch-house? A. By the persuasion of his brother, or somebody who came.
JOSEPH WATSON. I am a patrol of Hackney. On the 1st of November, I was on duty in Pigwell-field - there is a passage leading from there to a place called London-field, at the back of Grange's nursery - it is enclosed by a wall on one side, and a bank on the other - It is two hundred and sixty-four yards long; I heard a female voice screaming for assistance in that place, about half-past nine o'clock at night - I went to the spot, and when I got there I got on the bank, and saw the prisoner Smith with this young woman on the ground apparently attempting they were nearer to the wall than the bank; I got over the bank, collared him, and pulled him up, and told him he was ill-using the young woman - he made no answer at that time; I got the prosecutrix on her feet - she seemed verymuch fatigued, and was in liquor; she put her hand into her bosom, and said "I have not got it;" I asked her what it was - she said she had lost 5l.; I asked Smith if he had got it; he said No, Cattle had got it - and then she said Cattle had got it; the bosom of her gown was open, and her shawl laid on the ground; the pins were out of her gown, and her breast quite exposed - the gown appeared to me to have been torn open; Smith was very much intoxicated.
Q. When you came up, did you hear any thing? A.After I got over the bank, I spoke, and the young woman said she was robbed; she said she had lost her money; I thought I heard footsteps in the road; I went a few yards from Smith, in the direction of the steps; I met another patrol, and asked if he had met a man; he said Yes - I said "Well, bring him back, he has robbed a woman of 5l."- I spoke loud - I did not see the man; I then returned to Smith, and told him I should take him to the watch-house; he said I should not, or he would not go; I however took him there, and the prosecutrix went; I did not myself take Smith there, as another patrol came up at the spring of the rattle; he took him, and I took the prosecutrix there; two patrols overtook us on the road with Cattle - when I had been in the watch-house about ten minutes, I saw Cattle give Cowley a piece of brown paper with five sovereigns in it; he said the girl had given him the money to take care of: I did not hear any thing said before that, but my attention was particularly directed to the girl, who was in a fainting state - Smith gave me the name of Dick Barlow.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Were you near enough to hear what Cattle said when he produced the money? A. I did not notice - he was rather the worse for liquor, but not so bad as Smith.
Q.When she said her money was gone, Smith said Cattle had it, and she said "Yes, Cattle has it?" A. Yes.
JOHN CALLER. I am a patrol of Hackney. On the evening of the 1st of November I was on duty in the lane, and saw Cattle - I met him walking; I said "Patrol," and bade him good night; and after that I saw Watson fifteen or twenty yards behind me - I cannot say whether Cattle could hear what he said, as the wind blew away from him; he said something to me, and in consequence of that I went after Cattle, who continued walking till I got very near to him, and then took to running; I had said nothing to him then, but when he ran, I said, "You had better stop, young man," and sprang my rattle - he continued running: I ran, but could not overtake him - there are three paths on that spot; he did not take the path towards Margaret-street - he was a mile or two out of his way from Hoxton to Margaret-street; I sprang my rattle, and after that went back to Smith, Watson, and the prosecutrix, as I could not overtake him - on the way to the watch-house the prosecutrix began crying, as her petticoats fell off; Smith said,"Don't cry" - I said, "Have you got this young woman's money?" he said," No, I have not, Cattle has it - I asked her for it, and if she had given it to me I would have put it where no b-r alive could have found it;" two patrols overtook us with Cattle in custody - I said to Smith,"Then this is the man who has got her money;" he said"Yes, that is Cattle, he has got her money" - I do not think he was near enough to hear that said; I said nothing to him about the money in Cattle's presence - when we got to the watch-house I gave charge of them; Cowley asked me the charge - I said, "We have brought in these two young men, on suspicion of robbing this young woman of 5l.; Cattle immediately said "It is no robbery at all - here are the five sovereigns which she gave me to take care of," and gave them up directly to Cowley, the constable of the night, (who is since dead;) they were then locked up.
SAMUEL TREWBRIDGE. I am a patrol of Hackney. I was on duty on the 1st of November, between eight and nine o'clock at night - I heard a rattle spring, and saw Cattle coming towards me at a regular walking pace; I went up to him, and called him - he said at first," I am not the man, it is a young man and a young woman over in the field;" I said," I must detain you" - he said I had no right to detain him, and as he spoke I perceived he caught his breath;" I said, "You are out of breath, and I shall detain you" - he said I was doing him an injury in detaining him, and keeping him from his business; I said I did not wish to injure him - if he would go with me to the party, I would not detain him, if they said he was not the person; we proceeded across the fields - I do not remember his saying any thing more; he did not say a word about having any money - I took him across the fields, and met Gregory, the patrol, and left him in his custody; I went on and called Pilton, who came to me - the place is a narrow passage; I went back to where Cattle was, and heard Pilton say to him, "Cattle, you are the man who robbed the girl" - I do not remember his answer, but think he said,"How do you know?" Pilton said, "If I had not known you, how should I have picked you out from the rest;" a gentleman hearing this, said, "Now you secure him - secure his hands;" I proceeded towards Hackney watch-house with him in custody, and overtook Smith in custody near the Cock public-house - up to that time Cattle had not said a word about the money, but then something was mentioned that he had the money; the impression on my mind is, that he owned having it then - we went to the watch-house where Smith gave another name.
Cross-examined. Q. When Cattle said he was not the man, you had not charged him with any thing; whether it was for stealing money, or for assaulting a woman with intent to ravish her, he could not tell? A. No - I did not know myself; he said he had the money before he got to the watch-house.
JAMES GREGORY. I am a patrol. Trewbridge gave Cattle to me - he was in my charge for ten minutes or a quarter of an hour, and did not say a word about having any money: I did not go to the watch-house with him.
Cross-examined. Q.Did he say he had done nothing wrong? A. Yes.
JOHN PILTON. I am a patrol. I had Cattle in custody, and said, "You are the man who robbed the woman of the 5l." - he made no answer then; I took him down to the watch-house - when he saw the other prisoner he said he had got the money in his pocket, on his left-hand side; that was nearly a quarter of an hour after I had made the charge against him.
CATTLE - GUILTY. Aged 25.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Of stealing from the person only. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18281204-209]
Fourth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Baron Hullock.
209. JOHN BROWN was indicted for feloniously assaulting Emma Johnson, on the 25th of November, putting her in fear, and taking from her person, and against her will, 12s., her monies.
EMMA JOHNSON. I lodge at No. 12, Fuller-street, Bethnal-green, and am single. On the 25th of November, a little after eight o'clock in the morning, the prisoner came to my lodging; I was up - he knocked at the door; I answered him - I did not know him before; he asked me for a person named Johnson - I told him my name was Johnson - he said he came about the 4l. 9s., the robbery, and charged me with having robbed a gentleman in Cock-hill, Liquopond-street, the night before; there is a house in Cock-hill, where I occasionally sleep - he did not mention who kept the house; I told him he must be mistaken, I was not the person - he said Yes, he knew I was the person, for he had a description of me, as having a tooth out in front - I said I had two teeth out, and then went down stairs to the kitchen, to see my landlady; the prisoner, and a watchman named Reeves, followed me - the watchman had stood at the side of the door: the prisoner called him, when he came down stairs; I went into the back place - Brown and Reeves followed me; they shut the door - Brown desired Reeves to search me - previous to that I had taken 2l. 12s. out of my pocket, and had it loose in my hand; Reeves searched me, and took two sixpences out of my pocket - I dropped the 2l. 12s. out of my hand by accident; Brown called for a light, which a little girl brought - he picked up the money, and Reeves gave her the two sixpences he had taken from my pocket; we then went into the front-kitchen, and he told me not to be alarmed, that I was no prisoner, and he had not come to hurt me, that he had got what he wanted, which was the money; I told him where he was going, I would go with him; as they were both going away he said, if I knew when I was well off I should keep quiet and say nothing about it - I said I was innocent of any charge, and asked where he was going; he said he was going to see the gentleman on Cock-hill, whom, he said, had been informed I had robbed - I told him I would go with him; he said, if I would go I might, and that I could follow him, as I knew where he was going; I desired him to wait while I got my bonnet and shawl to go with him - I went up, put them on; we went out together, and as we went along he asked if I knew where they sold good gin; I shewed him a public-house - we had some gin there - I went into the gin-shop and had some gin with him, and Reeves - the prisoner paid for it; we then went straight to Cock-hill, and on our way they wished me to have move, but I declined till we got to Cock-hill - I had some more there; he then told me he had an appointment with this gentleman at three o'clock in the afternoon; I asked him where the appointment was - he said at his own house; I asked where he lived - he would not tell me; he said he would bring the gentleman to Cock-hill at three o'clock, if I would be there; I said I would, or else he said he would see me at eight o'clock, when he was on his beat - he is a patrol; he never came at three o'clock, for I sent, and went myself at eight o'clock and stopped till nine, but he was not on his beat; I never saw him any more till I saw him at the watch-house the night following - he said he had seen the gentleman, and returned him the money, and he gave him the 12s. for his trouble - I had gone to the watch-house to see for him; this was on Wednesday night, between eight and nine o'clock - he said he had got a receipt and could shew it to me, but he would not, and I then gave him in charge for robbing me.
Q. What did you take the 2l. 12s. from your pocket for? A. I thought it would be more safe in my hand; I did not give it to him - I tried to take it up when it fell, but he called for a light and prevented me from touching it, and took it up himself against my will; put it into his pocket, and took it away against my consent.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. I believe you are an unfortunate girl? A. yes; I understand he is a patrol - Reeves saw all the transaction - I have not taken any pains to bring him here; I have not seen him.
Q. For what purpose did you go down into the back-kitchen? A. I became alarmed, and went down to tell my landlady of it - I suppose he thought I was going to make my escape, and followed me; the landlady was preparing her breakfast in the kitchen - I went through there into the back-kitchen, that is not very dark, but is not so light as this place; there is a door opens into the light kitchen, and there is a little hole; there is a great deal of rubbish in the place; I swear the money fell out of my hand - there might have been a little rubbish thrown over it in the scuffle - I had passed part of the night before with a gentleman at a house on Cock-hill; the 2l. 12s. was given to me by a gentleman between eleven and twelve o'clock the night before - he was three or four hours in my company; he gave me no more than 2l. 12s.
Q. Do you remember, on the road, making any observation to him, about the woman who keeps the house on Cockhill having told of the robbery? A. No: I do not recollect any thing of the kind.
Q. Did you say "D-n her, I meant to have given her half the money if she had not nosed upon me?" A. Nothing of the kind - he did not say the gentleman lived sixty miles from town, and could not stop to prosecute me; he said he was to meet him at his own house, but at the watch-house he said he was to meet him at the Spread Eagle; I went to the watch-house on the Tuesday night, but did not find him till Wednesday - I have seen his wife repeatedly since this, and told her I did not want to hurt him, - I did not wish to see her and the family go to destruction; and she said I should have the money back - I did not say I would not appear against him: I was bound to appear.
ANN DOUGLAS. I am landlady of the house where the prosecntrix lodges; she ran through my kitchen, and the prisoner ran after her into the back place - she said nothing to me - she went through in a hurry into the dark place where the coals are kept - the prisoner demanded a light, a which my little girl gave him; I went in when the door was open, and saw Brown with 2l. 12s., which he said he had taken from the rubbish, which had been swept up there for the purpose of clearing the place; I heard a scuffle when the door was shut; Brown came out - he did not say before me why he took the money; I heard nothing about it being taken from a gentleman; he said he had no occasion to wait, as she knew where to come, and they left the kitchen- she went away with them afterwards; I do not know at what time she had come in that night.
Cross-examined. Q.How long has she lodged with you? A.About eight months; she had not told me she had received the 2l. 12s.; we were not particularly intimate - I had seen her that morning, she came down stairs first -Brown followed her; she did not speak to me, but went directly to the dark place; the rubbish was swept up behind the door - a person must go in and shut the door to get at it.
WILLIAM BILEY. I am a constable. On the 26th of November, between eight and nine o'clock at night, Johnson made this charge to me, and I took the prisoner Brown- (having been to his house, and brought him to the watch-house,) the girl stated the charge in his presence; he said he had paid the money to a gentleman at the Spread Eagle; I said "Then perhaps Brown you can point out the person at the inn who called the gentleman" - he said he could; Harper and I went with him to the Spread Eagle; he said it was a waiter at the tap that called the gentleman - we inquired at the tap, and found they kept no waiter - he made some excuse, and we went over to the house to find the gentleman there, but we could find no gentleman - a female at the tap said, in his hearing, he had made an application there that morning, and he was in company with a woman when he made the application, and that was at twenty-five minutes past nine o'clock: he said at the watch-house that the gentleman he paid the money to went off by the nine o'clock coach; I do not recollect that he made any answer to the female: we went to the tavern, and made inquiry, but could find no gentleman there who had been robbed; Brown was with us all the time; we then returned to the watch-house: he said he had a receipt which the gentleman gave him; the ward-beadle told him if he had the receipt, he should not be locked up that night; he said he would produce it to-morrow before the Magistrate, but would not produce it then; he was locked up in an hour or two, and remained in the watch-house till then.
Cross-examined. Q. You took him as soon as the charge was made? Yes - there are a great many cuds about the inns there.
Q. Do you expect a gentleman who had been robbed at a house of ill-fame would have told every body of the circumstance? A. No.
JOHN HARPER. I am the ward-beadle. I was with Riley at the watch-house, and the Spread Eagle, and corroborate his statement; I met Brown on the Wednesday morning, about ten o'clock, in Broad-street, and said "This is a very black job," and told him the circumstances of the case; the prosecutrix having been to the watch-house on the previous evening, and given information; he said "I have been down to Gracechurch-street, seen the gentleman, and made it all right;" that he had given the gentleman two sovereigns, and he allowed him to keep the 12s. for his trouble; I told him I hoped it was right, but if not, he would lose his situation.
SARAH ABRAMS. I live at No. 6, Cock-hill. Johnson was in the habit of coming to my house at times; she came there on Monday night, the 24th of November, at a quarter to eleven o'clock, with a gentleman; I was not at home, but my servant informed me so - I did not see her; she was brought there, between nine and ten next morning, by Brown and Reeves, and said she had been at my house the night before; Brown had knocked at my door, at a quarter to five o'clock that morning; I asked who was there - he said a gentleman in the first-floor had called to him, and said he was robbed; I said "If you will wait a moment, I will take a light of you;" I did so, went up stairs, and asked a gentleman in the bed-room what was the matter - he said he had been robbed of 4l. 9s.; I told him not to make an alarm; I knew the person who had been there well, but I could not tell what time she had been away; he dressed, and went down; I then let Brown and Reeves in; Brown said he was not satisfied but what the woman was in the house; I told him he was welcome to search it - he did so, and found nobody - Brown, Reeves, and the gentletleman went away together.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear the gentleman say he lived in the county? A. No; I was out when they came in - I came home at two o'clock in the morning - my servant is not here; my house is shut up at eleven; I described the prosecutrix to Brown, and told him where she lived.
Prisoner's Defence. Riley and Harper have an old grudge against me, and have taken this opportunity of using this against me, or they never would take notice of what an old prostitute, and well-known thief, have said - they have been trying for some time to spit a spite against me; the gentleman told me he did not wish to be exposed.
WILLIAM RILEY. I have no grudge against him.
JOHN HARPER. I have no grudge against him.
GEORGE REEVES. I am a watchman, on the adjoining beat to Brown. Mrs. Abrams made a communication to me respecting a robbery; I went with Brown to the prosecutrix - when Brown told her the charge, she ran down stairs into the back place - I got into the dark place two or three seconds after Brown, and I found her in a stooping situation, by the side of some dirt; the prisoner asked her where the money was - she denied knowing any thing about it; he called for a light, and said he considered the money was deposited in the dirt; I saw him find the money in the heap of dirt - it was covered all over with the dirt; there was not a piece of it to be seen before the dirt was raked over - it could not have been placed there by accident; she was close to the dirt when she was stooping; I was with Brown when the gentleman came out of the house; he said he was going sixty miles on the following day; when the money was taken out of the dirt, she asked him what he had got; he said 2l. 12s. - she then exclaimed that was all she had robbed the man of.
COURT. Q. What were her words? A. She said"That is all I took from the gentleman;" she said nothing, in my hearing, about the landlady of the house; I did not hear the gentleman call out of the window - I was not near enough; Brown called me - he has been a patrol about two years.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-210]
Before Mr. Baron Hullock.
210. EDWARD ROWLEY was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Holt, on the 1st of November, at Tottenham, and stealing there in, 1 basket, value 1s.; 1 salver, value 4l.; 15 spoons, value 6l.; 2 ladles, value 2l.; 2 salt-spoons, value 10s.; 1 meat-skewer, value 10s.; 2 knives, value 5s.; and 1 fork, value 2s., his property.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution
JANE ANDREWS. I am in the service of John Holt, Esq., who lives at Tottenham - he keeps the house. The plate in question is kept in a closet, in the body of the house, near the parlour door - the closet is in the passage which communicates with the hall; on the 1st of November, between one and two o'clock, I had occasion to go into the hall, and observed a strange man in the hall - I did not at first see any thing in his hand, but at last I saw he had the plate-basket which was kept in the closet; I did not see his face when he was in the hall; I saw the prisoner at Worship-street, on the 3d. but cannot speak to his person; he went out of the hall with the basket - I screamed out when I saw he had got the plate; I followed the man out of the hall to the gate of the fore-court, and saw Bramble, the footman there close to me; he passed me; I saw a horse and cart there, and a boy or man in the cart; I then saw the man with the plate-basket get into the cart to drive on; I followed it till the plate was given to me by a strange man; I did not notice Ray - Bramble followed the cart; I took the plate home, took it into the parlour, and then put it into its place.
EDWARD BRAMBLE. I am a footman to Mr. Holt. On the 1st of November, between eleven and twelve o'clock in the morning, I admitted some visitors; I shut the door after them, but omitted bolting it - I am certain it was fastened by the latch, and between one and two o'clock, I heard a noise, and went out to the fore-court - I do not know of any body having come to the house in that interval; I heard my fellow-servant scream in the hall; I was sitting in the kitchen, and saw here coming down the steps with the prisoner before her from the front steps I could see the steps from the kitchen; I went out at the side door to the gate, and pursued the prisoner; I saw him with the plate-basket in his hand - the prisoner is the man; there was a cart at the gate; I saw the prisoner get into it; he was driven off by a body who was with him; I was close to it when the prisoner got in, and pursued - I saw him give the boy the plate-basket; he took it, and put it down on the near side - as soon as the prisoner got one foot on the cart, the boy drove off at full speed: I was then laying hold of the tail of the cart; the prisoner then took the rains and the whip, and drove, and after a bit, be turned round, and began to horse-whip me; I had got hold of his coat: he then took the butt-end of the whip, and hit me on the hand, and over my head; I put my hand up, and he cut my hand, and two of my knuckles; he then threw the basket of plate out, saying"Take it you b-g-r;" I still continued to pursue him; I had hold of the skirt of his coat at that time - it gave way, and I have the piece here; it came off in my hand; I then let go of the cart, which went off at full speed; I have not the least doubt of prisoner's person.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You let the company in between eleven and twelve o'clock? A. Yes; I let in two ladies; my master has three servants in the house, also a coachman and gardener; he has a son and two daughters - I had not been out myself; whether any of the family had gone out and left the door unlatched I cannot say - the skirt of the prisoner's coat came off in my hand; I was rather confused, but am sure of him.
MR. BODKIN. Q.Have you the least doubt of him? A. Not the least; I took particular notice of him while he was whipping me,
WILLIAM RAY. I am servant to Mr. Talbott. On the 1st of November I was driving my master's team into Tottenham, and saw a cart coming along the road, with a man and a boy in it, and a person hanging behind - I saw the man throw the plate out of the cart - it all flew out of the basket about the road; I picked it up, put it into the basket, and gave it to a stranger, who gave it to the cook; I cannot swear to the prisoner.
WILLIAM TALBOTT. I am a farmer. I was on horseback in the Tottenham-road, and saw Ray, my servantand, from information, I went in pursuit of a cart, and come up with it between four and five miles off, at the end of Duvall's-lane, at Holloway; I road very fast, and met the cart; Holloway is out of the road from Tottenham to London; I went that way in consequence of information, through Newington and Islington to the Greenlanes; the prisoner, and a boy were in the cart - on coming up I held up a stick, I had in my hand and said,"Young man stop, I want to speak to you;" he said,"D - n you, what do you want with me, get out of my way;" and struck his horse; I turned my horse and road before him, and told him he should stop; he drove the wheel of his cart against the haunch of my horse, and by that means broke one of my traces; I still kept my seat, and began to cry Stop thief! he went a few yards further, then turned up a road out of the high-road - I still followed close at the tail of the cart; he went up the road about two hundred yards, then he and the boy jumped out of the cart together, and both went up a street - I passed the boy, and kept following the prisoner close all the way, and at last came up to him in a place which is no throughfare; he tried twice to get over a wall but could not - Chisnall came up directly.
Cross-examined. Q. Had the prisoner a coat or jacket on? A. No; he had disposed of the coat, he had a tight waistcoat with sleeves, such as I have seen many people wear; the cart was not in sight when I first started - I met it; some apples and walnuts, and two coats, were found in the cart - they were entire coats.
CAROLINE AKHURST. I am married, and live in Charlotte-place, Holloway. On the 1st of November I saw nothing of this transaction, but saw the end of a crowd, and picked up a silver dessert-spoon opposite to that place - I gave it to Morgan.
JOHN MORGAN. I am an officer, and produces a spoon given to me by Akburst; I took the prisoner, and found nothing but 2 1/2d. on him.
Cross-examined. Q. You found no picklock-keys? A. No.
THOMAS CHISNALL. I am a painter and glazier, and live at Holloway. I saw Talbott endeavouring to stop the horse, and noticed the man in the cart, and am certain it was the prisoner; I pursued as hard as I could, and saw them both jump out; the boy went up a passage, and the prisoner turned to the right.
EDWARD BRAMBLE. Here is the basket of plate - the value is above 10l. - they are all my master's property, and this spoon also; I put it all into the plate closet at half-past ten or eleven o'clock that morning, in this same basket.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of the robbery; I was coming down Holloway, a lad asked if I wouldhave a ride; he said another man had got out of the cart; I said, "Drive me as far as the turnpike."
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
Of stealing in the dwelling-house, but not of breaking and entering.
[trialAccount t18281204-211]
Before Mr. Baron Hullock.
211. WILLIAM VINE and WILLIAM BLUNDY were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Roworth, on the 6th of November, and stealing 50 yards of silk, value 8l, his property.
JOHN ROWORTH. On the 6th of November I lived in Castle-court, Bethnal-green. I went out about a quarter to six o'clock in the evening, leaving my wife at home: I returned about eight, and missed about fifty yards of silk which was in the loom when I went out - it was cut out; I had been working at it that day; I directly went to Worship-street and gave information; Armstrong promised to meet me in half an hour, but never came, and I got three watchmen to examine my place; there was a knife left in the room - the silk has not been found; it was up in the second - floor room where I lodge - there were three lodgers - Mr. Patrick lodges under my room, the prisoner Vine lived on the middle-floor, and a woman at the bottom; I sleep in the room the loom is in - we only have that one; we were down stairs in the lower room after missing the property - and about a quarter-past eleven o'clock we went up, and in a quarter of an hour Vine's wife came up, knocked at the door, and began saying what a sad loss it was; and while she was talking we found a knife, but whether it had been left there in the afternoon when their child was playing there I cannot say; it belonged to Vine - I have seen him at work with it several times; I found it by the fire-place close by the loom - I have lived there about a year and a half.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Vine has some children? A. One; it runs about, and used to play in our room - we occasionally lent each other things.
MARY ROWORTH. My husband left me at home; I went out at a quarter to seven o'clock and returned at a quarter to eight; I had locked the door and took the key; I left the silk in the loom - I returned before my husband and found the door wide open, and directly missed the silk and the shute also; I made an alarm, and the first person who came up was Mrs. Patrick, who lives in the bottom room; I found the knife on the ground between eleven and twelve o'clock - it was open; I had seen it in Vine's hands often; I never had it in my hand before, but am positive it is his knife; how it came there I cannot say.
Cross-examined. Q. Had not several persons come to the room after the alarm? A. Yes; Vine's child had been in the room the night before; he is a gold and silver wire-drawer.
JOHN M'WILLIAMS. I am a Bow-street patrol. The prosecutor gave me information about nine o'clock in the evening - I and two other officers examined the room, and found this brace-buckle between the chink-iron and the post of the loom; I examined the loom, and remarked, that if the silk had been cut out it must have been done by a left-handed man - it was cut at one side and torn across.
ROBERT TYRRELL. I am a Bow-street patrol. I was with McWilliams, and found the buckle - the silk appeared to have been cut out with a knife, as the place they had begun to cut was near the window, which would be very awkward to begin at with shears; I accompanied Neuson to the Feathers public-house, in Austin-street, about half-past eleven o'clock that night, and took Vine in the tap-room; I returned to the room, and the prosecutrix gave me this knife - I went next morning to Blundy's house, and asked if he had been with Vine the night before; he said he had been at the Queen's Head public-house with him; I told him to attend at Worship-street - his father said he should, and he came there; a buckle was missing from his braces - the one found corresponds with the one remaining; there is a piece of old black silk attached to them instead of the buckle - they are common buckles; the braces are old, and might have lost a buckle.
JOSEPH NEUSON. I was with the other officers, and apprehended Vine.
CATHERINE BURRELL. On the 27th of October Blundy came to my house to be measured for a pair of shoes, and as be stooped to tie his shoe I heard something crack - he turned up his waistcoat and fastened a bit of silk to the button; he had no buckle on his brace then: these are the braces he wore.
Cross-examined. Q. Then you know that nine or ten days before the robbery his braces wanted a buckle? A. Yes - I know the date, because I took my work home that day, and received 2l. 13s. 5d. for weaving ninety yards of silk; I have master's memorandum of it.
ABRAHAM KEYMER. I keep the Feathers - the prisoners were in and out of my house till about seven o'clock on the 6th of November. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-212]
Before Mr. Baron Hullock.
212. WILLIAM HUMPHREYS, GEORGE EDMEADS, WILLIAM PEAT and GEORGE MANNING, were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Pavey, on the 15th of November, at Staines, and stealing therein 3 shirts, value 4s.; 1 shawl, value 1s.; 1sheet, value 6d., and 1 pillow-case, value 6d., his property; 5 neck-cloths, value 5s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 1s.; 1 gown, value 5s.; 1 tablecloth, value 2s., and 1 yard of cotton, value 1s., the goods of Nathaniel Jones; 2 shirts, value 1s.; 1 watch, value 50s., and 1 night-cap, value 3d., the goods of George Hogburn.
MR. BARRY conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM PAVEY. I rented a cottage at Knowles-green, near Staines; the prisoner Humphreys lodged in a room there, with George Hogburn. On the 15th of November, about ten o'clock at night, I saw the house locked up safely, and went to bed - Humphreys had not come in - Hogburn was at home; I had locked up my house for the night - about the middle of the night I heard a noise; I lay still, and then I heard a little more noise - I got out of bed, looked out of the window, and saw a parcel of clothes lying on the ground, and two men there; they packed up some of the clothes, and ran away - this was at twelve o'clock; I examined the cottage, and found the glass of the window broken and the window up - it was safe the night before; I missed two shirts, a sheet, handkerchief, table-cloth, and pillow-case - someof them have since been shewn me by the watchman; I do not know the other prisoners - Humphreys did not sleep in the house that night at all; I found his hat and handkerchief in the cottage that night down stairs - Hogburn and he lodged in one room; I found a pair of Humphreys' shoes outside the cottage, and when he was taken he said they were his shoes and his hat, and he told me he was captain of the robbery, and mentioned the names of three others; his brother-in-law and sister were present: I rent the house, and live in it - there are two rooms up stairs and two down; Hogburn and Humphreys sleep up stairs, and so do I - I have no family but a wife; I saw nobody come into the house when I heard the noise - I found the glass of the window down in the back-room broken and opened; it opens with hinges - it was fastened when I went to bed, but by breaking the glass a hand could be put in.
GEORGE HOGBURN. I am a labourer, and lodged at Pavey's with Humphreys. I went to bed about ten o'clock on the night of the 15th of November - I expected Humphreys home that night; I heard a noise in the course of the night, and thought it was Humphreys coming up to bed quietly, that he should not disturb me- I had pulled my waistcoat off when I went to bed, and tucked my watch inside the sleeve of my waistcoat; I heard the steel seal gingle against an old coin which was fastened to my watch - I then suspected something was going on wrong; I jumped out of bed, and called, "Will, Will," three times - Humphreys did not answer; I suspected it was him; he ran down stairs without saying a word - I ran down stairs after him, and saw a person going out of the window on his belly; two persons outside were pulling him out: I caught hold of his legs, but they pulled him out - he had no shoes on when I caught hold of his legs; there was another man inside the room, standing on the top of an old bedstead while I was pulling his legs, and he hit me on the forehead-they got him clear away; then the other man and I had a tustle together - he caught hold of my throat, and pretty nearly strangled me; it was dark, and I did not know who it was, as not a word was said: after the struggle he jumped out of the window frame, and was pulled out; I lost my watch, two shirts, and a night-cap - my watch was worth about 50s.; it was silver: I examined the premises directly we got a light, and found that they had got in at the front window, they had gone to the back of the house, and took two squares out of the window-frame - I suppose they could not got in that way, but then went round and broke a glass, and opened the front-window by unhasping it; I found Humphreys' hat and handkerchief in the room where they entered, and I found his shoes outside the window - I know them to be his; he did not come home that night to sleep: I was present next morning when he was taken, and he did not deny the hat, shoes and handkerchief being his - he was asked if they were his, and did not say they were or were not; I think he was asked if they were his: he said he was there, and three others whom he named, were with him and told the constable where to take them: he said he came up stairs first, took my watch and gave it to Peat, who was in with him - I have not found it since; I have got one shirt and a night-cap from the watchman.
JOSEPH GREENER. I am a watchman of Staines which is about a quarter of a mile from Knowle's-green. Early on the morning of the 16th of November I met two men, but on the night of the 15th, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I met four men, who were quite strangers to me, and in the morning, between twelve and one o'clock, I met two persons in Staines - they said, "good night, watchman;" I said good night, and shortly after two others came up - one of them had a bundle under his left arm; I caught hold of it, and asked what he had got - he said his own property; he let go of it, and ran away: it was quite dark, and I do not know who they were - the prosecutor claimed the property in the bundle - I produce a shirt, which Hogburn swore to; I took all the prisoners in custody on the Sunday morning - I was sent for, and took Humphreys near Pavey's house, about eleven o'clock in the morning; he gave him in charge for breaking into his house - he asked me if I thought it would be of any service if he told who the others were, and from his information I took the the other prisoners - he said he was captain of the gang.
NATHANIEL JONES. I employed Mrs. Pavey as a laundress. I find in this bundle five neck handkerchiefs, two pocket-handkerchiefs, a cotton gown, a table-cloth, and a cotton print - my wife had sent them to Pavey; I cannot swear that from my own knowledge, but I know them to be mine.
GEORGE HOGBURN. There is a shirt and night-cap of mine in the bundle: they were in the house that night, down in the room where they got in. (Looking at the hat, shoes, and handkerchief) these are Humphreys' hat and handkerchief - they were found in the cottage, and these shoes are his; he wore the hat and handkerchief that day at dinner time - I knew it well before; I used to see him three times a day - I have no doubt of their being his hat and shoes; I know them, because the toes have flat pieces of iron to them; I found them outside the window myself.
HENRY NORRIS. I am in the employ of William Elston, who rents a stable. I know Peat - he is in the employ of Mr. Harding, a horse-dealer; Elston rents part of the stable, and Peat sleeps there - I locked him in on the night of the 5th of November; Mr. Elston came home after that, about twenty minutes after eleven o'clock, and Peat was gone away: I took Elston's horse to the stable, and he was not there - I saw him next morning, about ten minutes to seven o'clock; I saw his bed - it had not been slept in that night, and he looked as if he had been up all night, I said, "Bill, why, where have you been to?" he said he had been out, but he had soon returned after I had been to the stable and not found him.
GEORGE LAYTON. From Humphreys' information I assisted in apprehending the other prisoners.
HUMPHREYS - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 17.
EDMEADS - NOT GUILTY.
PEAT - NOT GUILTY.
MANNING - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-213]
Second London Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
213. WILLIAM PITT was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of November, 1 gross of coloured galloons, value 10s.; 5 gross of double ribbons, value 4l. 2s. 6d., and 5 gross of other double ribbons, value 5l. 10s., the goods of Sibley Foster and another.
EDWARD SMITH OWEN. I am in the galloon trade, in partnership with Sibley Foster, and live in Goldsmith-street. On the 1st of November, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, I sent a lad with a parcel to the Bell and Crown inn, Holborn - it contained the articles stated in the indictment.
SAMUEL MOORHOUSE. I am errand-boy to the prosecutors. On the 1st of November I had this parcel to deliver at the Bell and Crown, and was going down Skinner-street - the prisoner stood outside Muncaster's (the pawnbrokers) window, and called to me; he asked if I would go and fetch him a coach - I had another boy with me: he said he would give us 6d. a piece - he gave us 1s.: I put my bag down, and said to the little boy, "You mind the bag," and while I was going I met the little boy by the Saracen's Head; I had been absent four or five minutes: the boy told me to bring a chariot - I immediately ran back to where I left my goods, and the prisoner was gone, bag and all; this was on Saturday - I saw him on the Monday morning, and knew him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Of course you pointed him out the moment you saw him? A. Yes - I saw him come out of the lock-up place; the officer met some gentleman on the Saturday night, in Gracechurch-street, but I did not think right to go near them; I do not know whether that was the prisoner - I did not see his face - I was about three yards from him, but was not looking that way; we were going along and met him - I was about a yard behind the officer; the officer tapped him on the shoulder, but I did not see him do that - he stopped, and talked to him, and then I went away; it was rather dark- I did not hear what passed, and did not know it was the prisoner.
Q. Then you were looking that way? A. They were looking towards Fenchurch-street, and I towards Bishopsgate-street; I described the person who robbed me as being above forty years old, and of very low stature; I had never seen him before, to my knowledge - I went to St. Andrew's watch-house, and gave information directly, and about half-past five o'clock saw the officer tap the gentleman on the shoulder; I was robbed about four.
COURT. Q. What do you mean by a man of low stature? A. He was not tall.
ENOCH JACKSON. I was with Moorhouse; a man stood at a door in Skinner-street, by Seacoal-lane, and a man at the top said, "A man wants you there;" we went to him: he said he would give us 6d. each to fetch a coach - I staid by the goods, and he said to me, "Go and tell him not to fetch a coach but a chariot, and make as much haste as you can;" I went, and when we came back the bag and man were gone; we went to the watch-house directly - I left the bag with the prisoner; he is the man.
Cross-examined. Q.Had he the same clothes on then? A. No, he was dressed in black - he had black trousers on; I never saw him before - I was keeping my eye on the goods: I was with the prisoner about nine minutes.
Q. Have you been repeating your story to any body? A. No, Sir - Yes, Sir; nobody told me to say it was nine minutes - the man appeared about forty, but not more, and of a low size; nobody told me to say he was about forty.
JURY. Q. What age do you think the prisoner is? A. About forty, and of low stature.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you not cry, and tell your father next day that you were not there at all? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Why did you tell your father so? A. I was afraid he would beat me.
THOMAS SHELSWELL. I belong to Lambeth-street office. On Saturday night, the 1st of November, I apprehended the prisoner at a public-house in Rosemary-lane, and on Monday these two boys said he was the man.
JOHN THOMAS GRAY. I am an officer. About a quarter-past four o'clock on Saturday a lad came to St. Andrew's watch-house, and said he had been robbed; I went in pursuit, and met two persons in Aldgate High-street that night - the prisoner was one; he said, "Mr. Gray, how do you do?" I said, "Very well, thank you," and walked away; the boy might be a yard or two off: I went to the office, and gave information.
Cross-examined. Q. He knew at the time you were looking for the robber? A. Certainly - he was walking in the same line with me; he might pass the prisoner face to face - he might be a few feet behind, but he must have passed him; it was near a shop window, with a gas-light to it.
NOT GUILTY.

214. JAMES ABBOTT and THOMAS KILLICK were indicted for a conspiracy.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-215]
Fifth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
215. JAMES BARRY was indicted for feloniously assaulting William Perrott, on the Hing's highway, on the 19th of October, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 hat, value 1s. 6d.; I handkerchief, value 6d,, and 1 staff, value 1s. 6d., his property.
WILLIAM PERROTT. I am a watchman of St. Luke's. On the 19th of October, about half-past one o'clock, in the morning, I was on duty, in Bunhill-row, and walked round Chiswell-street, to Grub-street, where I saw ten or twelve young men standing; I walked up to them, and said, "Young men, you should not stand here at this hour;" and before I could say any more, one of them knocked me down - they were all together, at the corner of Broad Arrow-court; I cannot say which of them knocked me down - the prisoner was one of those who stood among them, I am certain; as soon as I was knocked down, one of them got behind me, and put the cape of my coat all over my face - while one of them held the cape over my face, another wrenched my staff out of my hand, and leathered me with it; he beat me with it - I do not know who did it, because the cape was over my face - they then robbed me of my hat, and handkerchief, and left methere; Hogwood, the watchman, came and picked me up - my hat was on my head, and the handkerchief in it; it would not fall off very easy; it was taken off, and carried away - the blow, he struck me on the side of my head, made me insensible; I had a severe blow on the head - I looked down Grub-street, at the left hand, and saw a young man turn up a court; I followed him - when he got to the end of the court, I lost him, and when I came back, I saw the prisoner, in custody of Sackerson, with my hat and handkerchief in his hand; I said, "That is the prisoner - he is one of the men who were together when I was knocked down;" I am certain he is one of the party, but who knocked me down, or took my hat, I cannot say - I had a watch in my pocket, and about 1s. 5d.; that was all safe, because I had my jacket buttoned.
Prisoner. This is very wrong - I only picked the hat up; I never put my hand near him.
RALPH HEARN. I am a brass-turner, and live in Grub-street. On the 19th of October, about half-past one, o'clock, I was sitting up for my lodger; I heard a rattle spring, and went to the door - the watchman was bringing a man along; I heard a scuffle among the Irish people at the corner of Broad Arrow-court - I went over; I saw the prosecutor, and observed them give him a violent blow - I saw him fall; another one put his foot across him, and began kicking him - I immediately went for assistance; I cannot swear to any particular man. for I ran to fetch a watchman - I met Sackerson, and sent him into Grub-street; he ran - I ran on to the watch-house, to fetch more watchmen, and as I came back they had the prisoner.
JOHN HOGWOOD. I am a watchman. A man was given into my charge, about one o'clock, on the 19th of October -I was going to the watch-house with him; Hearn came to the watch-house, and fetched me - I went down to Grub-street, and saw Perrott lying on the pavement; I assisted him up - he was quite unable to tell me the cause of it; I left him leaning against the public-house, and went in pursuit of the prisoner, whom I was given to understand had gone down the street, with the hat; I followed a short distance down Grub-street, and met Sackerson about fifteen yards from the spot, coming back with the prisoner and the hat.
WILLIAM SACKERSON. I took the prisoner; I met him coming out of Silk-street, about one hundred yards from where this happened - he had the hat in his hand, and as soon as he saw me, he put it behind him; the handkerchief was in it - I rather think he had not found his way out of the street, and was returning; I collared him, and took it from him.
JOHN COMPTON. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house between one and two o'clock; he said he picked the hat up in the kennel.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. This is not the handkerchief that was in the hat when I picked it up - it was an old one, not worth a penny; it was a small bit of a handkerchief; I was at work till twelve o'clock that night - I went and had a pint of beer, and as I came along Grub-street there was not a soul in the street; I saw the hat in the gutter, and took it up - I went about five yards, and stood at the corner of Silk-street, with the hat in my hand; the watchman asked what I had - I said, "A hat," and gave it him.
WILLIAM PERROTT. This is the very handkerchief I had in my hat that night.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-216]
216. DANIEL CARTER was indicted for feloniously assaulting Amelia Woodroffe, with intent to rob her, and her goods and monies from her person violently and feloniously to steal.
AMELIA WOODROFFE. I live at No. 17, Denmark-court, Strand. On the 26th of November, about half-past twelve o'clock at night, I had been to the English Opera-house; I went, also, to Drury-lane Theatre - I was coming back to the English Opera-house again, and, as I came out of Drury-lane Theatre, I saw the prisoner, in Bridges-street, talking to a woman who had lived servant with me; I crossed over from the woman, and was going into the oyster-rooms - a man came up, in a white fastian jacket, he took me round the waist, and asked if I wanted a coach; I said No, and with that the prisoner at the bar came up, and said, "What is it o'clock?" That was the first time I saw him; I said, "I don't know," and then the one in the white fustian jacket said to the prisoner, "It is of no use, it is a duffer's watch" - (I had had no conversation with the woman and the man; she had left me on the Friday, and this was Monday) the prisoner said, "Never mind, we will have it;" I then crossed over Russell-street, towards Bow-street, to get from them - the prisoner then came up and said, "What is it o'clock?" I made him no answer - this was in Bow-street; both of them had followed me - I did not see my servant again; I went into Bow-street - the prisoner came up to me again, pulled my cloak aside, and took the watch in his hand, but it was supported by two watch-books and a tape; it hung it my belt - he turned it round; the other man was ten or fifteen yards from him at that time - that man gave a whistle, I think with his fingers in his mouth, by the sound, and the prisoner caught hold of the watch; I called Watch! the watchman came up and took the watch out of his hand; I still retained possession of it - the books still held it to my belt; he said to the watchman, "It is of no use taking me - it is only a duffer's watch;" the watchman laid hold of it at that time - he asked the watch-house-keeper to allow him to speak to me; he then gave me a desperate blow, and knocked me down in the watch-house - I had a desperate black eye; the Magistrate would not believe he had knocked me down, but he said himself that he had; he said, "Will you appear against me to-morrow?" I said Yes, and he gave me a desperate blow; I never saw him before, till I saw him talking to my servant, nor had I seen the man in the fustian jacket before.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How many names have you? A. Clara Fisher is the name I go by, because I do not wish to disgrace my parents, by going by my right name; I was living with Mrs. Belasco - she keeps a brothel in Denmark-court; my servant's name is Louisa Ward - she was living with the prisoner at that time - she is no rival of mine, for I had never seen the prisoner before, she was servant to Belasco; one or two ladies live there - I occupy the first-floor, and another lady the other; there are dress lodgers and lodgers in ordinary - I was a lodger in ordinary; the watch was my own - I gave twenty guineas for it, and that woman knew it; I had had it between twelve and eighteen months - I have been at Belasco's nearly two years; Mrs. Belasco was not with me when this happened - one of the books which fastened my watch is lost, and the other broken; the prisoner followed me for about a quarter of an hour - the watch was not to be seen until the man came up, and took me round the waist; I crossed over to Bow-street to find somebody to get hold of him.
Q. Was there any difficulty in getting the watch if he was disposed? A.Decidedly; because I held the seals, and pulled the watch, or he could have got it - I pulled the stone out of one of the seals with holding it; I will take my oath it was in his hand when the watchman came up; Ward told the Magistrate that it belonged to Belasco.
FRANCIS TAYLOR. I am a watchman. I heard theprosecutrix call Watch! I was a few yards off - I came up; the prisoner, and a good many more stood in the street - I suppose five or ten stood together; the prisoner said "Watchman, it is of no value, it is only a duffer, not worth 5s. - I don't call it any thing to be given in charge for;" I asked if she insisted on the charge - she said Yes; she charged him with attempting to take her watch, and breaking the guard from her side; I took him to the watch-house, and he there hit her on the bridge of her nose, and knocked her down.
Cross-examined. Q. About what o'clock was this? A. After twelve; I sprung my rattle when I got him in charge - there were two of them together; one wore a fustian coat; she said he was one, and so I sprung my rattle - I have not had the watch; she had hold of the seals, and he said to me, taking hold of the watch,"Watchman, it is only duffer:" I will not swear whether he had hold of it when I came up - I saw it in his hand, but did not see him take hold of it; I now know where Belasco lives - I have been there for Woodroffe three times: I did not know her before this: she has kept the watch herself - the Magistrate ordered it to be given to her.
COURT. Q. What does duffer mean? A. I asked him what he meant; he said it had no works in it; I never knew Belasco's house before this happened - it is not in my parish.
Prisoner's Defence. I hope you will put the watchman on his oath whether he thinks this is spite or not - it is nothing else; I have known the woman ever so long, but lately have not spoken to her; and seeing me talking to Ward, she and Belasco took and followed me, took hold of me, looked me in the face, and let me go; I said to Ward, "These young women are following us;" they came and stood round us; the watchman came by - she said, "I give charge of this young man:" he said, "What for? I won't take him unless you say the charge," and she said, "He has attempted to take my watch:" I have known her these nine months.
FRANCIS TAYLOR re-examined. She said he had attempted to take the watch from her side; I did not give her time to tell me before I asked her what it was about- I cannot tell whether it is spite or not.
LOUISA WARD. I lived at Belasco's four months; it is a house of ill-same - there was a dress lodger there besides the prosecutrix; I was in Belasco's service, not in hers; this happened on the Tuesday after the Friday on which I left; I met the prisoner in Brydges-street - he stopped and spoke to me; he walked on - I stopped to tie my patten; she and Mrs. Belasco came out of Mother H.' oyster shop - I heard her say to Mrs. Belasco, "Here he is, so help me God I will;" (I have often heard her mention the prisoner's name, and say she would send him out of the country if it laid in her power) the other young woman said, "No, don't Clara:" she said," I will;" she gave Mrs. Belasco her muff, opened her cloak, then took a brass watch which she wears, and gave it to Mrs. Belasco, who took off her gold watch, and gave it to her; and, before they could get to Bow-street, Mrs. Belasco stopped and took hold of this young man by the collar, and then let him go again; Mrs. Belasco and Clara then ran round him and began a quarrel with him for the space of five minutes; she called the watchman, and insisted on his taking charge, and when he was taking him to the watch-house Mr. Belasco went up to him, and said, "Let him go:" the watchman said he must do his duty - I did not go into the watch-house, for Mr. Belasco heard me say I would speak for him, and he wanted to give charge of me; I went away, but went before the Magistrate the next day, and stated what I have here.
COURT. Q. Had there been any quarrel between Fisher and the prisoner? A. Not that I know of; they never associated together or cohabited; I cannot say why she wished to send him out of the country, for I never heard - she meant transportation no doubt; I saw her change the watch, and then she charged him with attempting to take it - she never had a gold watch in her possession before; she was only trusted with the brass one - I suppose she was ashamed to swear a robbery against him for the brass watch; he did not attempt to take it from her - he was never near her; they followed him - she gave him in charge at the end of Bow-street, she followed him all that way, and then called the watchman; I was behind her at the time.
MR. CLARKSON to FRANCIS TAYLOR. Q. Has any body given you any money since you have been in Court? A. No.
Q. Not a farthing? A. Oh, yes: I did not know what you meant; the prosecutrix gave me 1s. to get my dinner, because she said the London cases were coming on- it was within a few minutes of five o'clock; I had had no dinner, and was going to get it, but was afraid I should be wanted; I swear it was only a shilling, and here it is - nobody said any thing to me about going to drink with the prisoner's friends.
WILLIAM HENRY HEWETT. I live in Portland-town, Regent's-park. I am attending here on another case: while standing among the people yonder, my attention was attracted by some conversation between the watchman and prosecutrix: I heard her reprimand him for keeping company with the prisoner's friends over the way, or at least out of doors, and at the same time she gave him 3s.; I can be positive there were three sixpences, and I thought it was a crown, but am certain of 4s. 6d., at the same time saying, "If you want drink, here is money to get it, but till the trial comes on keep yourself sober?" there was something so particular about the admonition, I thought it right to mention it.
COURT. Q. You are here on another trial? A. Yes: a sheep-stealing case; I am a carman and carrier - I will swear to the money put into his hand; I only know the woman as the prosecutrix.
Q. How came you to know her? A. By the party standing in Court; she said, "What did you go among those out of doors for? there is a great many of them, and I don't want you to be along with them." making use of an observation; she put the money into his hands - she suited the action to the word; I am quite uninterested in the matter - I never saw any of them before.
FRANCIS TAYLOR. On my oath I have received no more than 1s., and that she gave me for my dinner - I have not had a farthing from her for drink.
AMELIA WOODROFFE. On my oath I gave him only 1s. - they have threatened me; my life is in danger; this witness came and asked me to give him something to drink:I said, "I don't know who you are:" I brought out 7s. 6d. - here are 6s., which he saw in my hand; that man is one of the gang - they are a set of the vilest wretches.
Q. You cannot say he is one of the gang if you never saw him before? A. I saw him with the prisoner's friends at a public-house - there are twenty of them outside; he came and said to me," Will you treat me?" I said, "I don't understand you," and I gave the watchman 1s., and asked a man at the door if the trial would come on to-night; he said he thought it would.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-217]
217. JAMES MOTT & MARY ANN BARRAND were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Andrew Carney, on the 16th of November, at the Liberty of the Rolls, and stealing therein 7lbs. of cigars, value 6l.; 14lbs. of tobacco, value 50s.; 32lbs. of snuff, value 6l.; 84 snuff-boxes, value 5l.; 12 pipes, value 30s.; 2 pairs of scales, value 16s.; 5 brass weights, value 4s.; 1 brass image, value 7s.; 1 cigarbox, value 1s. 6d., and 1 basket, value 2s., his property.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
ANDREW CARNEY. On the 16th of November I lived in Bishop's-court, Chichester-rents, Chancery-lane, in the Liberty of the Rolls - I was the last person up that night, and saw the house all safe; I kept a snuff-shop there; among other things there, I had a brass image of Napoleon Buonaparte, gilt in oil, and fixed on a pedestal; also a box of cigars, with a label on the front of the lid of the box; about half-past seven o'clock next morning my son alarmed me - I went down, and found my cellar-door broken open, and in the afternoon I found a crow-bar in the cellar; I found the partition of the shop broken open, three short pipes on the counter, and a small piece of candle and a bunch of matches, some of which had been burnt; I missed all the articles stated in the indictment; I gave information, and afterwards saw the image of Napoleon Buonaparte and the cigar-box in the officer's possession.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you any other Christian name? A. No; nor any partner.
COURT. Q. How had they broken into your house? A. Into the cellar, and then through the partition into the shop; the underneath part of the shop was secure the night before - we never open it; we had only been in the house four weeks - the partition was safe; I lost property amounting to 23l.; it is my dwelling-house.
JOHN NORRIS. I am a Police-officer. In consequence of information on the morning in question, I went to some lodgings, in which I found the female prisoner, and the male prisoner was sitting getting his breakfast; my brother officer went into the room first, and said, "Halloo, Joe! your name is Joe Batt, is not it?" he said Yes; (we had no pistols) - my brother officer said, "We are too late I suppose;" he answered, I think, "I am afraid not," or, "I believe not;" I turned my eye under the bed, and found a large shawl full of cigars - my brother officer found a gilt image of Napoleon, and I found a cigar-box filled with snuff, there was no top to it, and a basket was found; I shewed the cigar-box and image of Napoleon to the prosecutor at the office - he claimed them; the top of the box was produced and fitted the box.
Cross-examined. Q. It would fit any box? A. It might; I was never at the woman's lodging before; the man behaved remarkably civil and quiet - we told him he was a prisoner, and he went without resistance; I did not know him before, and cannot say whether he lodged there.
THOMAS SHELSWELL. I found some picklock-keys in the room where we found the prisoners and the property; I stepped into the room and said, "Your name is Joe Batt?" as I understood that was his name; he said it was; I said, "I am afraid I am too late;" and he said,"I am afraid not;" I took him in charge; Norris said,"Oh, here is the property, it is all right;" (taking it up) -Mott said nothing to that.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know these lodgings? A. Yes; I was never there before - I did not know the female prisoner before, nor how she got her livelihood.
Q. Do you know that her door is open all night? A. I only know it was open when I went up.
JOHN NORRIS re-examined by MR. CLARKSON. The female prisoner lives there; I believe it is a house let out to girls of the town by a man named Wedderburn; I cannot say whether the room-door is open at night - I was never there before.
Q. Any person who might have cohabited with her that night would have been found as he was? A. Certainly.
ANDREW CARNEY. I know this image of Napoleon, and have no doubt of it - here is a pin broken off which fastened it at the bottom; I know the cigar-box, here is the maker's marks on it which were on my box - and this cover, which was not taken, fits the box; there are three strokes extending from the cover on to the box, and every one of them fit; I lost 7lbs. of cigars, and this reticule-basket, is what I lost: the crow-bar was found in my cellar in the afternoon of the robbery.
MOTT'S Defence. I never said any thing about being afraid he was too late.
Two witnesses gave the prisoner Mott a good character.
MOTT - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 23.
BARRAND - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-218]
NEW COURT, Fifth Day.
Fourth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Common Sergeant.
218. JOHN FINN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of November, 1 spoon, value 10s., the goods of Thomas Prideaux, his master.
THOMAS PRIDEAUX. The prisoner was my apprentice: I live in Princes-street, Cavendish-square. On the 12th of November, between seven and nine o'clock, this spoon was missed: I asked the prisoner about it the next morning, and he denied knowing any thing about it; I have the fellow to it, and have no doubt it is mine.
MARY COWDRAY. I am nurse in the family. I missed the spoon, and told my master.
WILLIAM PACKER. I am a pawnbroker. This spoon was pawned with me on the 12th of November, between six and eight o'clock, by the prisoner - I am certain of him.
HENRY STOWELL. I am an officer. I took the prisoner - he said he knew nothing about it.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw the spoon.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18281204-219]
219. JOHN PATRICK was indicted for embezzlement. MR. BARRY conducted the prosecution.
