WILLIAM PEARSON. I am an upholsterer, and live in London-wall. I had a shop at the corner of Catherine-street, Strand - the prisoner entered my service about four years ago, and was authorised to receive money on my account, and was to render an account monthly; I had a customer named Sheldon; I have examined my monthly accounts, and find I have not received, since January, 6l. 4s. on his account.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. He came into your service four years ago? A. Yes, and was to receive 2l. or two guineas a week; I never recollect any conversation about his being allowed a commission on what he sold, nor about his receiving any thing at Christmas; I did not say I had allowed it to his predecessor, and would to him; this 6l. 4s. was not included in the monthly accounts; I recollect receiving an account from my wife - this is it; here is credit given for 6l. 4s., received from Mr. Sheldon, deducted from an annual gift of 20l. - he does account for 6l. 4s. in this way; I issued a writ against him for the money before he absconded, and then took out a warrant; I never gave him permission to go out of town after rendering the account - his brother came and brought a receipt, which I said was no account at all; Mrs. Pearson gave the prisoner a receipt- she handed an account to me, in which he had made an account for 80l. as a gratuity - his brother did not give me any account; I issued a writ against the prisoner directly.
MR. BARRY. Q. When he went away, you brought this forward? A. Yes - I thought the embezzlement was complete - here is the account for January 1828 - the 6l. 4s. is not down; he should have rendered an account of it the last day of the month; he used to send the account sealed up to the warehouse - not to the house.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Is there not in this account an item of 10l.? A. Yes, I made him a present of that; I swear I did not give it to him on account of a gratuity; I very often make my servants presents - it was in June 1826; I think I saw him but once after I received this account from my wife; he stayed on my premises two or three days, but evaded me in every kind of way; I called several times, and could not see him.
JOSEPH SMITH. My daughter, Matilda Sheldon, was a customer of Mr. Pearson's; she directed me to pay the prisoner 6l. 4s., which I did, in January last, for Mr. Pearson- I had a receipt, but I got wet yesterday, and changed my clothes, and came without it.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose it is by looking at the receipt only that you speak to the time? A. Of course.
COURT. Q. When you looked at the receipt, did that refresh your memory, and enable you to swear it was paid in January? A. Yes.
ELIZABETH PEARSON. I am the wife of William Pearson: the prisoner was in the habit of rendering his accounts to the clerks, or to Mr. Pearson, but never to me before; in August last, Mr. Pearson pressed for his account, and I think at Mr. Pearson's desire, I wrote to him; the prisoner brought me this account - I could not check it; I never opened the paper - I was going to give him an acknowledgment for the sum handed to me, which was 16l. 8s. 5 1/2d. - he said he had a receipt ready written - I signed it without reading it; when my husband came home, I handed him the money and the account - I am not conscious that the seal was broken.
Cross-examined. Q. Then you are in the habit of assisting your husband in his business? A. No - I have hand money handed over to me by the clerks, which I have paid to Mr. Pearson, or sent to the banker's; I have received money, and given an acknowledgment for it; it was in consequence of a letter I sent to the prisoner that he brought the account- our establishment in the Strand was about to be given up.
Prisoner's Defence. At the time I entered Mr. Pearson's service, in 1824, I was to succeed Mr. Robinson, whom I had some knowledge of - he agreed to give me 100l. a year, but it was a verbal agreement - Mr. Robinson said he did not give commission, but he made a present of 20l. a year at Christmas - Robinson is now dead; I had repeatedly asked Mr. Pearson to settle - he said "I am in a hurry, and can't give it you now;" in June 1826, I asked him to let me take 10l. on account, which he allowed me to do; Mr. Sheldon knowing me, became a customer of Mr. Pearson's, and this sum I held back, considering myself entitled to it; on Thursday, the 22d of August, I was desired by a note to bring the book to London-wall, and Mr. Pearson found it all correct - there is a lad here, I believe, that can prove it - this is the book, and here is the account I rendered - he took the book. and said "Mr. Patrick, you received money of Mr. Dodd," and then he marked them all off in the book, and made no objection - on the Monday following, he called on Mr. Pearson - two or three persons had been inquiring if the premises were to let; when he called, I told him of it - he said I must go and tell them 300l. rent, and one thousand guineas for the lease; I went to several persons, and one of them appointed to meet Mr. Pearson, at London-wall; on the Wednesday evening, I met him at the factory, and went to London-wall with him, and when he was gone, I was ushered up stairs to Mr. Pearson, and this account lay before him; Mr. Pearson had a sale on the 26th of August, and from the 22d, I had never seen him - I wrote a note to know whether I was to shut up the shop or not - then a man applied for it to exhibit the Red Barn, and he took it for a week - on the 27th, I saw Mr. Pearson, and asked him leave to go into the country; he gave me leave, and then he asked for the receipt - I sent my brother with it the next morning, when he picked out two sums of Clementi and Frieseland, and said "I will issue a writ against your brother," and he took out a writ that every morning; my brother came back to the Strand, and I went into the country; I came back on the 9th of September, and wrote to Mr. Lacey, to say if he had any instructions to take proceedings against me to send to Austin-friars, and on the 18th of September, he took out the warrant against me; I was at Carpenters' Hall several times - I never absconded; I supposed, had he intended to take any proceedings, he would have sent to Austin-friars; here is the mark in the book against this very account, and Mr. Pearson made no objection; he says he did not see me, but he saw me on the Monday and on the Wednesday evening.
MR. FRY. I served a notice on the prosecutor and hisattorney yesterday, to produce a certain account-book, and a letter, bearing date August 20, 1828.
SAMUEL TAYLOR. I left a letter, of which this is a copy, with Mr. Lacy, the prosecutor's attorney; he admitted so to me - I took it from the prisoner.
Newington, September 9, 1828,
SIR: - If you have instructions from Mr. Pearson to take any proceadings against me, I shall take it a favour if you will send them to Mr. Coates, Austin-friars; if any, have taken place, I will call, with two friends, and sign a bail bound. J. P.
MR. BARRY to MR. PEARSON. Q. Is the account in your hand-writing? A. This date is not mine; this letter is Mrs. Pearson's.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Was this letter written by you after the account given to Mrs. Pearson? A. I think I can swear it was before, to the best of my knowledge it was two days before this account was furnished to Mr. Pearson - supposing there to have been no entry of four years gratuity, it would not have been a correct account, because he had delivered a July account, in which he had omitted items which he credits in this account; he gives credit for them here: -(Letter read.)
SIR: - Having been out all day, I have been prevented from answering your note sooner. Mr. P. being from home, I feel at a loss what to say; but I think to-morrow you may as well open the shop as usual; the lots can be cleared, and, agreeable to Mr. P.'s instructions, I shall be glad to have your account, and, on Mr. P.'s return, I will hand him your account for his immediate attention.
August 20, 1828. ELIZABETH PEARSON.
MRS. PEARSON. I do not know the date of the receipt.
MR. PEARSON re-examined. Q. Did you compare the account with your book, as the prisoner states? A. This green book is a book of sales, not of money; it is merely of goods sold - this account is the one he delivered, sealed up, to Mrs. Pearson; this other, is the cash-book, which was rendered to me after he delivered the account - I compared it with the cash-book, and it was fabricated; the prisoner kept this book, and when I saw it, three or four days after, it was not made up - I can prove the monies are entered as paid, in August, when they were received in May, July and February; he writes them here as paid in August, and in the former leaves they are never credited.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Do you mean that in that green book there are not entries the same as in the account? A. Yes, of sales; I have never compared the entries in this book with the account rendered by the prisoner, and ticked them off.
JABEZ PATRICK. I am the prisoner's brother. On the 28th of August, I went to Mr. Pearson, to exchange a receipt of the 21st of August, signed by Mrs. Pearson; it purported to be for the balance of the account - I went to give it up to Mr. Pearson, and to have one of his own; I understood, from my brother, it was at Mr. Pearson's request, and to take some vouchers for monies paid to Mr. Pearson; Mr. Pearson was at breakfast - I sent up a note, and was asked to sit down; I had the receipt in my hand, and gave it him - he said, "As your brother is going into the country, it is necessary I should have these vouchers;" he had a stamp lying on the table, which he had commenced writing before I got there, as the ink was quite black; he finished it, and took up the receipt I gave him, and handed to me the one he had written - this is it; it purports to be for two specific sums in the account previously delivered to Mrs. Pearson; I was going to hand him a receipt for 102l. 5s. 4d, for salary, and he said, "I do not want that- there is the receipt for the two specific sums of Clementi and Frieseland, and I will arrest your brother for 80l. immediately;" I was going to make some reply, but he seemed in a great rage - I did not know what had been committed, and I came away.
MR. BARRY. Q. What are you? A. An accountant; I am in no particular employ at present - I recollect my brother leaving Mr. Pearson's service; I did not receive him into my house - I gave the receipt to my brother.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-220]
220. JOHN PATRICK was again indicted for a like offence.
JOHN TODD. I was a customer of Mr. Pearson's. On the 29th of May, I paid the prisoner 10l. 4s. 6d. for Mr. Pearson; he gave me this receipt - (reads.)
Received, on the 29th of May, 1828, of Mr. Todd, Ten Pounds Four Shillings and Sixpence, for William Pearson.
J. PATRICK.
JOHN LANGFORD. I paid the prisoner 4l. 14s. 6d., on the 11th of July, for a mattress; he gave me a receipt, which I gave the person I paid the money for; I am sure I paid the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Do you know of a sale taking place at Mr. Pearson's? A. Yes - but it was before the sale, I believe, but I am not certain; the prisoner was at the sale, and received the money - I was there; it occasioned a great deal of confusion.
WILLIAM PEARSON. I did not receive 10l. 4s. 6d. on Mr. Todd's account, nor the 4l. 14s. 6d. on Mr. Nelson's; they should have appeared in the May and July account - the 10l. 4s. 6d. was for goods sent into the country, and I questioned the propriety of sending them; he (the prisoner) said it was a very respectable gentleman, and he had no doubt it would be paid - I asked for it several times, and requested him to write to Mr. Todd for the money, and he declared from time to time he had not been paid.
Cross-examined. Q. Are not these two sums inserted in the account delivered to Mrs. Pearson? A. They are; he never before made up an account purporting to be an account of money received at the sale - he had nothing to do with the sale; the auctioneer had.
Prisoner's Defence. The reason there was no account rendered in July, was, the sale was on the 26th, 27th, and 28th, and they were clearing the lots; Mr. George bought three lots, and I sold several - we had no goods, only some lots which were bought in; I sold one to a friend, who has not paid me yet, thought I paid over the money to Mr. Pearson - Mr. George is here, and you may ask him whether he did not buy the lots from the knowledge he had of me.
JOHN GEORGE. Immediately after the sale, I bought some lots from the prisoner; he was there, managing the things which remained unsold - I paid him for the lots I bought.
Mr. PEARSON re-examined. Q. Did the prisoner sellany lots after the sale? A. Yes - but I have not had the money for them; I asked the prisoner for the 10l. 4s. 6d., as I happened to be in want of money.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-221]
Third Middlesex Jury. - Before Mr. Recorder.
221. EDWARD ROSE was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of November, 1000 bricks, value 1l. 13s., the goods of William Mountford Nurse.
MR. ALLEY conducted the prosecution.
JOHN DARKE. I am a wharfinger at the Junction-canal, Paddington - the prisoner was in my service; Mr. Nurse, the builder, employs me to receive his bricks, which are burnt at Action. On the 17th of November, I received one thousand for Mr. Knott, and delivered them to the prisoner, about a quarter before six o'clock; they were all in one cart, with two horses - they were Mr. Nurse's; I saw about one thousand bricks in the Alpha-road, on the 22d; Mr. Nurse has two marks on his bricks - one is W. N., and the other N.; I never authorized the prisoner to take any bricks there.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. It was not light at that time? A. No - there are fifty or sixty men employed on the wharf, and about thirty carts; there are two moulds; the bricks are all marked so at that place - there are millions made there; I have sold a great many.
JONATHAN HILL. I live in Little James-street, Pomona-place, Lisson-green, and keep a horse and cart. The prisoner applied to me, on the 17th of November, to go round by the Nightingale public-house, and fetch up some bricks, and bring them to Steven's-buildings, for Mr. James - I removed them at two loads, as I had but one horse.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you were taken up? A. Yes - when the Magistrate was about to commit me, I told them; there were about one thousand bricks; I showed Mr. Darke the place I took them to - I do not know that Mr. James was there when I shot them; when I took Mr. Darke there, the building was carried up - there was no building when I went.
MR. ALLEY. Q. Did you take any bricks out of the wall? A. Not then, but we have since, and I have them here; they have the mark on them.
WILLIAM HILL. I am the son of the last witness. The prisoner called at my father's house, and paid me 3s. 6d. for the removal of the bricks - I helped to load one load; my father carted them.
PETER CARROLL. I am a labourer, employed by Mr. James, to whom these bricks were sent - these bricks were taken out of his wall.
COURT. Q. Was the wall fresh built? A. Yes: I cannot tell how long ago - I am not very often there; Mr. James lives in York-mews, nearly a mile from the wall - I was not there when the bricks were delivered; I was there about a fortnight before - I do not think the wall was built then; the foundation was laid, it was level with the ground; I took this brick from the wall this morning and washed it, to bring it decently to Court.
SAMUEL HUNTER. I am contractor for Mr. Nurse's bricks - these are made for him.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know whether Mr. James has bought any of him? A. No.
MR. DARKE re-examined. Q. Had any of these bricks been sold to Mr. James? A. No: they all pass from my wharf - one thousand would be worth about 33s. - Mr. Nurse's name is William Mountford Nurse.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Confined Three Months and twice Publicly Whipped.
[trialAccount t18281204-222]
222. THOMAS FISHER DODSON was indicted for embezzling, on the 20th of September, 1822, 40 sovereigns and 20 half-sovereigns, which he had received on account of Frederick Woodbridge and others, his employers.
This offence having been committed before the passing of Mr. Peel's Act, in the absence of all proof of any specific coin having been paid, the prisoner was ACQUITTED.
[trialAccount t18281204-223]
223. JOHN BARCLAY HILL was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of November, 15 lbs. weight of pepper, value 18s.; 13lbs. weight of ginger, value 16s.; 1 1/2lb. weight of cloves, value 6s.; 1 1/2lb. weight of Spanish liquorice, value 2s.; 1lb. weight of mace, value 7s., and 1 bag, value 3d. the goods of William Eastman and others; and SAMUEL SHERRIN was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute. (See page 80.)
HILL pleaded GUILTY. Aged 28.
Judgment Respited .
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM EASTMAN. I am a partner in the firm of Eastman and Co. - there are two other partners; Hill was employed by Baily, a carman in our neighbourhood - Hill lived at No. 31, Paul-street, Finsbury, and Sherrin at No. 85, Paul-street, exactly opposite - our employ of Hill took up the whole of his time. In consequence of something Hill told me, I went to Sherrin's on the 11th of November, about half-past eight o'clock in the evening, with Roberts the officer, and my partner Mr. Mayne; Sherrin was at home - on first going in I asked him if he knew a person named Hill; he hesitated for a second or two, and I think he said No, but I will not be certain as to that - I said,"Hill, over the way, at No. 31;" he then said Yes, he did; I asked if he bought any articles of him - he said he had; I asked what articles - he said ginger, cloves, mace, pepper, allspice, and juice, which is the name in the trade for Spanish liquorice - he said all that in answer to the first question, and said he believed he was buying them in a fair way, because they were wrapped up and made up in a tradesman like manner; I asked if he had invoices - he said he believed he had had invoices; I asked him to produce one - he said he could not put his hands upon them, or they might have been destroyed; I said it was a very untradesman-like way of taking in goods, and asked if the ginger I saw in a glass in the window was what he took in of Hill - he said, part of it; I then asked how much ginger he bought of him - he said three pounds; I said I thought the top of that glass was what he bought of Hill - the top part which I distinguished was near upon three pounds I think; I then asked if that was all he bought of Hill - his reply was Yes; I looked further into the window, and saw another glass containing about four pounds of ginger, corresponding with the top part of the other glass - I then asked where he got the bottom part of the first glass; he said he bought that of some person in St. Mary Axe, but would not tell me who - I said it looked very strange; I asked if he had any objection to shew me the other spices he boughtwith the ginger, and he shewed me some cloves, mace, and pepper - I asked how much pepper he had bought of Hill; he said three pounds, but there was some in the drawer before - there was about three pounds and a half in the drawer which he shewed me; I asked if he had any more pepper - he said he had not a bit more pepper in the house; the officer then went behind the counter, and took a bag from under it, containing eleven pounds and a half of pepper - I knew the bag to be one of mine; the prisoner was very much agitated - the officer told him he had better shut up his shop, as he must go with him: he gave no account of how he got that eleven pounds and a half; I saw the cloves and mace - there was one pound of mace, one pound and three-quarters of cloves, and one pound and three-quarters of Spanish liquorice; the cloves and mace were in a drawer, the juice was in a glass - when the officer brought him out he wanted to go as far as Hoxton, but he was taken to the Compter: he said it was a bad job, and hoped I should not be hard with him, or words to that effect - I am sure that is the sense of the words he uttered; I had never in any way commissioned or authorised Hill to sell any goods for me at any time - the fair price in the market for ginger at that time was about 1s. 8d. I was selling to trademen for more than that in a wholesale way - I have stated the price of pepper at 1s. 3d., but it was worth 1s. 4d. to sell in like manner - cloves were worth 4s., mace about 8s., and juice about 1s. 2d.; Hill had one room only in the house where he lived - I cannot say whether I went up two flights of stairs or one, but there was no shop or any appearance of business; the price of nutmegs at that time was from 5s. 10d. to 6s. 2d.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. Had you known Sherrin before? A. No. It would not be easy to identify Spanish liquorice, cloves or mace; those things which I could not have identified he produced - he stated how Hill had introduced himself to him, and said he sold things upon commission; the money was to be paid to the persons for whom he sold, and he had 1d. or 2d. in the pound, as the thing might be - that Hill had represented to him that his employers would sell the articles as good and as cheap as he could get any where; 1s. 6d. would be a fair price for the pepper, and 3s. or 4s. for the cloves; I believe I was talking of one of his dealings with Hill, and he said, that at that time he bought 3lbs. of ginger - I then asked if he had bought any more; he denied buying any more: I am not going to swear to the ginger - he said he had not bought more than 3lbs. of pepper - that he had never bought more than 3lbs. of ginger of Hill; I have not the least doubt the pepper found in the bag was mine, but I cannot swear to it - the bag is here; I can swear to that: I cannot state that it was in my possession at the time it was stolen - I do not recollect his saying it was a had job for him to be taken into custody; I never said to him, "Don't be alarmed - you shall not be hurt," nor any thing to that effect; I made him no promise of any kind: I believe I did say, "We shall deal no harder with you than the case requires" - that was in answer to what he said; we were at that time conveying him to prison.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did Sherrin tell you the name of Hill's employer? A. No; he did not state he had ever asked him - he said he had dealt with him more than once, and had bought some nutmegs of him, but we did not find any - a man in business might have 1s. 6d. per lb. for such ginger as this; no man would have given 9s. per lb. for cloves - he might have given 9s. per 1b. for mace, but if the cloves and mace had been mixed in equal quantities, the value would have been reduced; he said he never bought but 3lbs. of pepper of Hill, that in the drawer was the article, and he had no more in the house - I was present when the bag of pepper was found under the counter; It was rather a concealed place, I should think: in going to the Compter he spoke first to me.
COURT. Q. Immediately before he said he had no more pepper in the house, had he said he had bought no more than 3lbs. of Hill? A. I believe he did - all our conversation, decidedly, related to what passed between him and Hill - I did not go behind the counter, but I understand there were many parcels: when this further quantity was found he did not say "I did not buy this of Hill"- I said I had no doubt it was mine; he did not say a word to that; I stated before the Magistrate that I told the prisoner I had no doubt the pepper in the bag was my property, and all the other articles.
JOHN ROBERTS. I am an officer of the Mansion-house. On the 11th of November I went with Mr. Eastman to Sherrin's shop; I went in with him, and Mr. Eastman asked him if he knew a person of the name of Hill-he said he did not; "What (says he) not Hill over the way?" Yes, he said, he did - Mr. Eastman then asked him if ever he bought any thing of Hill; Yes (he said) he had - Mr. Eastman said, "What might it be?" he said 3lbs. of ginger, and he pointed to a shew-glass which it was in - he then lifted the glass to the counter; it contained 9 1/2lbs. of ginger: he said, "The top part of it. 3lbs., is what I bought of Hill, and the remainder of a person in St. Maryaxe;" Mr. Eastman said "What price might you give for it?" he said, "A very good price;" Mr. Eastman said,"Did you have a bill with it?" he said Yes - Mr. Eastman said, "Could you produce the bill?" "No (he said) I cannot - I believe they are destroyed; I don't know what's become of them:" on looking round further Mr. Eastman saw another show-glass in the window, with 4lbs. of ginger in it - he said, "Halloo! there's some more of my ginger there," and then asked the prisoner if he bought any pepper - he said, "Yes, I bought 3lbs. of pepper of Hill:" we said, "Can you shew it?" "Yes (said be) I can," and produced a drawer with 3 1/2lbs. in it, and said,"There was a little before I put the 3lbs, in;" he was asked if he had any more pepper, and said, "No, I have no more in the house at all;" I then found a bag under the counter, with 11 1/2lbs. of pepper in it - there was a little bag of rice, and several other things there; I put the bag of pepper on the counter, and Mr. Eastman said,"That's my property - I can swear to it;" the prisoner made no reply whatever - some juice, mace, and cloves were produced; the prisoner said he bought the greater part of them of Hill - I told him he must shut up his shop and go with me, and I took him into custody.
COURT. Q. Were there any other persons in the shop? A. No - there was a young girl in the parlour adjoining, but he was the only person in the shop serving; I took him to the Compter, and he said to Mr. Eastman, "I hope, Sir, you will be as lenient as you possibly can, as I have given the best price for the goods;" Mr. Eastman said, "Icannot answer any question whatever - you must go before the Magistrate."
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You went to search the house? A. Yes; Mr. Eastman pointed out the shew-glass with the 4lbs. of ginger - the prisoner said he had bought 3lbs; I am quite certain he did not say ginger, cloves, mace, allspice, and juice - I recollect that I am upon my oath; he first said ginger, and afterwards he went on with the other things; in answer to the first question, whether he bought any thing of Hill, he said 3lbs. of ginger, and that was produced before he mentioned the others; he never said he had bought cloves, mace, allspice, and juice, till we found the other things; he never said, in answer to Mr. Eastman's general question, whether he had bought things of Hill; that he had bought ginger, pepper, allspice, mace, and juice: when Mr. Eastman saw the bag, he said "That is my property;" that was before it was opened, and of course what he spoke of related only to what he could see; I never heard Mr. Eastman say he would be as lenient as the case would allow - he did not tell him not to be alarmed.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. As soon as the bag was produced, Mr. Eastman said "That is my property?" A. Yes, it was opened, and there was pepper in it; the prisoner did not then say it was what he bought; the pepper is here, and the ginger, and other articles; the prisoner said he bought ginger, and he then stated the other articles; I have stated all that I heard pass in going to the Compter -the prisoner and I walked arm and arm, and I think I must have heard what passed.
MR. EASTMAN. This bag contains 11lbs. of pepper - it is marked H. and Co. - that is not the mark of our firm, but I believe we bought all that were sold of them at the Victualling-office, Debtford- it is an unusual bag to send pepper out in; I would not have sent pepper out in it; I had never sold any thing to the prisoner - there are many different sorts of pepper - our shop has different; I do not, for a moment, suppose this was taken at one time; I have no doubt I have pepper of these different qualities - some of the ginger is mixed, and it is difficult to separate; I have selected a few pieces from that which was in the first glass, which decidedly corresponds with that in the second glass; we have often missed goods from our stock.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. The bag is not proper to carry out pepper? A. Certainly not; I bought these bags with some others which were more serviceable. but these were of very little use - I might use them for wiping a lamp, or any other dirty purpose; we might have put ginger into them; the pepper is of different qualities - other persons who deal in pepper may have the same qualities; I believe an application was made for the prisoner to be bailed.
An affidavit made by the prisoner, Sherrin, for the purpose of being admitted to bail, was read, in which he deposed that he had known Hill about four months, and about two months ago he called at the deponent's shop, and represented that he had these articles to dispose of for a respectable house with whom he then lived; in consequence of which, he ordered, at different times, 3lbs. of ginger, 3lbs. of pepper, 1lb. of cloves, and 1lb. of mace, which were brought in about a week; that he paid 1s. 6d. a 1b. for the pepper, and 9s. for the cloves and mace; and that upon the prosecutor calling, he related the whole transaction to him.
Prisoner's Defence. I must evidently be to blame, because I must have been misled by the man; he came to me, and told me he was commissioned to sell goods, and was alallowed 2d. in the pound - I gave him an order, not thinking I was doing wrong, and when I received the goods, I little thought any thing of the bill, as he said he was employed by the house - I considered that I was doing a fair and upright transaction; I had no reason to suppose any thing else; if I had, I should not have done it; I have been seventeen years in the house.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-224]
224. JAMES HALL was indicted for bigamy.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS PARKER. I am a surgeon, and live in Seymour-crescent, Euston-square. The prisoner resembles(the person, and, to the best of my knowledge, is) the person, who was married at St. Pancras New church, to Rebecca Martin, in 1822 - I attested the marriage, and acted as father to the bride - I have not seen her since.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. Have you seen the husband since? A. No; to the best of my knowledge and belief, the prisoner is the man; I can almost swear positively to him; I believe he is the person; I cannot swear positively to any thing.
SARAH MARTIN. I am sister to Mrs. Hall; her name was Rebecca Martin; I saw her last Sunday week.
Q. Did you see the prisoner living with her as her husband? A. Yes, for some time; I only knew of his visiting her once while single; I saw him three or four months before they were married; they lived together as man and wife somewhere about six years.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. Where is Mrs. Hall? A. I do not know; I have not seen her since Sunday week, when she called on me; I did not often visit her while she was living with the prisoner - I was in the country three years of the time; I think I saw them living together half a dozen times before I went into the country, and I have seen them since.
SARAH DENNEY. I was married to the prisoner at Islington, on the 22d of November last; I had some property - he got 100l. of it; he stayed with me till the next Wednesday morning, and was taken up that evening - I had not the least idea that he was married before.
HENRY BECK. I was apprentice to the prisoner; I knew his wife - her Christian name was Rebecca; they lived together till the 10th of November - her relations visited him; I have seen Sarah and Ann Martin there; I have seen my master write - I believe the signature to this register to be his writing.
COURT. Q. Where did he live, and what business did he carry on? A. A cabinet-maker, in Walbrook-place, Hoxton New town.
SARAH MARTIN. My sister can write - I know her hand-writing.
MR. BRODRICK. The register has Rebecca Martin's mark.
OCTAVIUS YOUNG THISSELTON. This is my writing, and Rebecca Martin's mark - she made the mark (producing the register.)
MR. BRODRICK. Q. Are you parish-clerk of St. Pancras? A. Yes; I wrote this attestation of her mark - she made the mark because she could not write her own name.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q.Have you not often known that persons who can write make this mark? Yes, it occurs nearly every day.
THOMAS EUSTACE. I am clerk to an attorney. I knew the prisoner, but not before be married Rebecca Martin; the prisoner gave me instructions to prepare a deed of separation between himself and his wife - she was named Rebecca, the wife of James Hall - her maiden name did not appear; this was in October last - he was living near Goswell-road; he never shewed me a certificate of his marriage.(Certificate read.)
Marriage solemnized in the parish church of St. Pancras, Middlesex, 1822 - John Hall and Rebecca Martin were married by banus, the 1st of July. By me, W. H. CHARLTON.
WILLIAM MERRY. I took the prisoner at the Wellington public-house in Goswell-street-road - I heckoned him out - he trembled exceedingly, and said what was the matter; I charged him with felony, and when I got him out, I said it was for marrying two wives; he said,"Can't it be settled?" I said "No; you must go before a Magistrate;" he went on about two hundred yards, and said, "I will give you 10l. a piece to let me go;" and a few yards farther he said, "i will make it 5l. more;" he said, "What can be done?" I said."I have the documents in my pocket;" "Then (said he) I am done, I shall be transported."
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q.Who was with you? A.Sanders; (who I did not then know was an officer) - the money was not offered that we might take him to the watch-house, and not to prison.
RICHARD SANDERS. I am a constable. I was present when the prisoner was taken - he wanted to settle it, and to take us to some house; we told him we could not settle it, we must take him to Worship-street - about two hundred yards further he offered us 10l. a piece; we said we could not take it - he then offered 5l. more.
DANIEL DOBELL. I am a boot-maker, and live at No. 42, St. John-square - I was at the Wellington public-house when the officer came; I followed the prisoner out and heard the conversation pass - he said he would give them 10l. if they would allow him to be locked up in the watch-house, and not take him to the New-prison Clerkenwell; they said they could not.
Prisoner's Defence. I offered them 10l. to treat me kindly, and not put hand-cuffs on me - I knew well I could not get away from two officers; I have no wife in existence but this lady, (Mrs. Denney) - I never left my home.
GUILTY. Aged 37.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-225]
Fifth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
225. THOMAS CONDON was indicted for embezzlement.
WALTER DOUGLASS. I am a stone-mason. The priprisoner and I were both employed by one master - we were at work at the new church, Chelsea; I employed him to receive money for me - I used to keep the men's time, and send an account of it to him; on Saturday, the 8th of November, I wrote out the bill of time of all the men that were there, and gave it to him - he was to take it to Ann Sharpe's yard - her executors carry on the business; my time was six days, which came to 30s. - he was to go to the foreman and get the money - he never paid it to me; I never saw him again till that day week, when he was brought back from Bristol - I believe Mr. Stukes has the bill I sent; he left the men in a miserable state, they had no money to go on.
JOHN STUKES. I was foreman to Mrs. Sharpe. On the 8th of November the prisoner came to me with this bill, and I gave him 4l. 12s. 6d. for the wages of the several men, including his own money - he ought to have given it them within an hour; their names are, Douglass, Wright, Bishop, Dawson, Coleman, Hughes, Daley, Collins, Cunningham, and Owen Hughes; I gave the prisoner this piece of paper, specifying the sum due to each man - it was found on him.
- WRIGHT. I was employed with the prisoner, and was to receive 30s. on the 8th of November; I never saw him till the Saturday following.
JOHN BISHOP. I worked at the same place - my wages were 1l. 10s. - the prisoner was to have received it and paid me; I never saw him till the 17th.
JOHN DAWSON. I was employed at the same place - I was to have 30s.; I never saw the prisoner till the 17th.
- COLEMAN. I was employed in the same way -I was to have received 18s. - the prisoner never paid it me; he had paid me once before.
- HUGHES. I was employed there, and was to have received 18s. - the prisoner never brought it to me.
OWEN DALEY. I was employed there, and was to have 18s., but I did not.
DAVID COLLINS. I was employed there, and was to receive 3s., but he never brought it to me - that Saturday was my first day; I saw Douglass give him the paper to go for the money - he was to have brought it to the Crown public-house, close by the Westminster-hospital, but did not come.
TIMOTHY CUNNINGHAM. I am a labourer. I was to receive 18s. - the prisoner did not come to the public-house.
OWEN HUGHES. I was to receive my money at the Crown, but the prisoner did not come to the house.
DANIEL DAWKINS. I am an officer. I took the prisoner at Bristol, on Friday, the 14th of November - I told him what for; he said he got drunk, got into the Park, lost half of the money, and was ashamed to come back; I found this paper and 4s. 5 1/2d. on him.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18281204-226]
206. THOMAS BIRD was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of December, 1 gown, value 5s, the goods of Elizabeth Smith, from her person.
ELIZABETH SMITH. I am single. On the 3d of December I was going from town to Kensington, and had a gown in a parcel in my hand - I was on the other side the turnpike at a quarter before ten o'clock at night; the prisoner came and snatched it from me with violence and ran off - I never lost sight of him; I am sure he is the person.
JAMES LARTER. I saw this lady crying Stop thief! the prisoner was coming down the foot-path - be broke into the road, threw down a parcel, and ran towardstown; I sprung my rattle - he was stopped by three of the Royal Horse Guards; I took him from them.
GEORGE LIDDELL. I am a constable. I bring this parcel, which was brought to the watch-house.
CALEB-HALL. I was returning from town, I heard a female voice cry Stop thief! I turned and pursued the prisoner - the two Horse Guards had got him; I took and delivered him to the watchman - I did not see him throw any thing down.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-227]
227. WILLIAM PERRIN, WILLIAM TURNER, and JAMES JOHNSON, were indicted for feloniously ripping and cutting, on the 2d of November, 2 cwt. of lead, value 2l., the goods of Samuel Brooks, and fixed to an unfinished house.
THREE OTHER COUNTS, varying the manner of stating the charge.
MR. SMITH conducted the prosecution.
JOHN LEE. I was employed as private-watchman on the premises of Mr. Brooks, in St. Paul's-road, Ball's-pond, Islington. On the morning of the 2d of November I was on duty, and about five minutes before two o'clock I heard something - I went up against the houses which are building by Mr. Simmons, I heard a chopping of lead on the top of the house, and something falling at the back of the premises - I got some other watchmen, we surrounded the house; I proceeded to the back, where I saw three or four men standing over this lead, folding it up, some of them were stooping down - I caught Perrin, who was one of them - he staid with me a quarter of an hour, and then made a great resistance to getaway, but another watchman came up and he was secured, all the rest got away - two or three of them went inside the building; I was not able to discern who they were; I afterwards saw Johnson and Turner brought out of the house in custody; I have compared this lead with the roof, and it fitted exactly to the width of the gutter - I saw the end of the gutter the day before, but I did not go on the roof; I did not go up again till the 5th of November, and then I fitted the lead - there are five pieces of it.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. You compared the width of the lead? A. Yes; these houses were building by Mr. Simmons or Mr. Brooks, I cannot tell which - I believe Turner has been employed in the buildings; I never saw the other two; Perrin said they were taking away the lead by Mr. Simmons' order -I had seen the prisoner at a public-house at eleven o'clock; they asked a boy, who was with me, to go in and drink.
JONATHAN HICKS. I know the premises in question -I saw these houses on the Saturday before, but was not on the top till the morning the lead was taken.
HENRY KINGSBURY. I am a watchman. I was called to assist Lee - I went into the back part of the premises, and saw three persons standing over the lead; one man got away, and two got into a house - I followed them into the house, which was unfinished, and when I came back from the watch-house I took Turner into custody.
DAVID RICHARDS. I went on the top of the premises about three o'clock, I climbed up inside one of the houses - I went on four houses, and on the fifth I saw Turner and Johnson lying on their backs, with their feet in the gutter; I called out "You are prisoners-surrender quietly;" they made no answer: I said I would fire on them - they then jumped up, and said, "You dare fire?" and ran over four houses; I said it was in vain for them to attempt to get away, as there were watchmen there - they then said they would give themselves up; I asked what made them cut the lead up - they said Mr. Simmons would not pay them their wages, and he told them to cut the lead up, and make money of it.
JOHN MORGAN. I am a solicitor, and am attorney to Mr. Brooks - he holds this land under the Marquis of Northampton; there are unfinished houses on it.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you undertake to swear they are Mr. Brooks' houses? A. Yes - he makes advances; he cannot take them away himself.
WILLIAM BLAND. I am a surveyor, and live at Brunswick-terrace, Islington. I was employed at this place by Mr. Brooks, and was to pay in advance on the buildings in question; I have seen the prisoners on the ground - there were other buildings as well as Mr. Simmons: I have not seen the prisoners on these houses.
SAMUEL BROOKS. I am proprietor of the ground at Ball's-pond. The prisoner Turner worked for Mr. Simmons - Perrin worked for his father, who is building five houses on the same ground, and Johnson for his brother, who is building nine houses there.
PERRIN'S Defence. I had been at work on the ground from the time the buildings began; I have sometimes worked for my father; I had worked the last fortnight for Mr. Simmons - when I went for my wages. he went to Mr. Brooks, who would not advance him a farthing, and he was in danger of his life among the labourers; we got him away - Mr. Simmons directed us to go and get this lead, and put it into his lock-up house, to pay the men with what money could be got for it.
JOHNSON and TURNER made the same Defence.
ROBERT SIMMONS. I have lately been building some houses on Mr. Brooks' ground; there were lead gutters upon two of them - Mr. Hancock supplied the lead, and I paid for it; the prisoners were all in my employ - I remember the Saturday night when this occurred; I have reasons for it: the men applied to me for their wages - I had just paid the money out of my pocket for the lead, and I went to Mr. Brooks for money to pay my men, according to a verbal agreement, but he refused to give it me; I told the prisoners to get down this lead, and I would make money of it on the Monday morning - they went a little too late.
MR. SMITH. Q. Are you of opinion they went a little too late? A. Yes - they had a right to have gone when I ordered them, and then they might have got the money on the Saturday night; I did not know when they went till now.
JOHN JOHNSON. I am a bricklayer, and live at No. 8, Queen-street, Islington. I was at the Royal George public-house on this Saturday night; I heard Mr. Simmons come in and say Mr. Brooks would not advance him a farthing - he told them to go and take the lead off, that he might make money of it to pay them.
MR. SMITH. Q. Is not one of them a relation of yours? A. Yes, my brother; but I have been in London, and he in the country; I have had him under my employ four or five years - I cannot tell what time I was at the public-house, I suppose about six o'clock or half-past; I remained there till ten, in the same state with my men as Mr. Simmons was - I could get no money - when I left I went to my own house: I do not know where the prisoners went - I left them at the public-house.
SAMUEL PERRIN. I am the prisoner's father. I was at the public-house, and heard Mr. Simmons give the prisoners directions respecting the lead; he said he could not get money to pay their wages, and to go and take the lead off, which he had paid for, and lodge it below in his premises.
MR. SMITH. Q. Do you know that he could not get money? A. Yes; I was in the counting-house when he asked for it and could not get it - I could not hear what was said, there was such a noise; but I was outside when Simmons came out and said he could get no money. I am sorry to say that Mr. Brooks is not a regular man in his payments.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-228]
228. JOHN WALKER was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of December, 1 jacket, value 10s., the goods of John Simonds.
JOHN SIMONDS. I am a seaman. On the 1st of December I came on shore from my ship, at five o'clock at night- I left my jacket and other things in my hammock; I employed a man to go for them, and he employed the prisoner - they were brought to King James-stairs, by the man; I did not see the prisoner then - I saw the hammock was undone, and I said to the waterman, "Something is amiss - you had better go back;" he put back, and we told the officer of it - the officer took the prisoner, and the duplicate was found on him.
RALPH LANCASTER. I was employed to go on board the Peggy, and get the hammock - the prisoner (whom I have known from a child) went with me to Greenwich; we could not get up, and we went on shore at Deptford -I gave him some grub; I then went to the Peggy, and the mate gave me the things - I brought them to King James-stairs, and saw the prosecutor; I left the prisoner in the boat - he then came on shore: I said to him, "I promised you 6d., and I will give you something more," but he was very shy, as he had helped himself - the prosecutor and I then went into the boat; he saw the hammock was undone, and said, "Avast here. Ralph, shove back - my hammock has been unlashed;" we put back, but I could not find the prisoner - we told Mr. Wilson, who found him.
Prisoner. Q. Was I not on the stair-head when you put off? A. Yes.
WILLIAM WILSON. I am a patrol. I took the prisoner on the night of the 1st of December, in the White Hart public-house; I told him I wanted him for stealing a sailor's jacket - he said he had had no jacket, and knew nothing of it; he said, "I will go down with you to Lancaster," and in Farmer-street he tried to get away, but he fell down, and I took him to the watch-house - I found this duplicate on him.
WILLIAM CANE NOOK. I am a pawnbroker. I gave this duplicate for this jacket; it was pawned on the 1st of December, between six and seven o'clock at night, but not by the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. The duplicate was given to me, and that is all I know; the person said, "You have got a very poor jacket on your back - I have been pawning one for 3s.; here is the duplicate."
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-229]
229. HENRY RICHARDSON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of November, 1 wheelbarrow, value 12s., the goods of James Wrigglesworth.
JAMES WRIGGLESWORTH. I lost a wheelbarrow some time before the 4th of November; one of my men found it that day in posssesion of the officer.
JOHN BARNES. I bought the barrow of the prisoner about nine weeks ago.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Did not he bring another man with him? A. Yes - but the other man did not say it was his; the prisoner told me he had an old barrow to sell - he did not say for another man; I paid 4s. and a pot of beer for it, at the Dublin Castle public-house, Park-street - I laid the money on the table; I do not know which took it up: the prisoner bargained to sell it to me.
WILLIAM COLTON, I found the barrow in possession of Barnes, at the Scotch school, in the New North-road- he said he got it from Richardson, and I took him.
WILLIAM BARTLETT. I understood that the prisoner was wanted - he was taken, and asked what I wanted with him; I said on suspicion of stealing a barrow - he said,"That barrow which Barnes the gardener had of me?" I said, Yes - he said, "I found that in one of the shrubberies in the Regent's-park - I sold it to Barnes for 4s. and a pot of beer.
JOHN WRIGGLESWORTH re-examined. My barrow was lost from the brick-field, in the road near Holloway - it is worth 10s. or 12s.; the place was trenched round to keep strangers out - he must have got it at night.
GUILTY. Aged 41.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18281204-230]
230. ALEXANDER MUNRO and JOHN MORGAN were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of October, 2 pairs of shoes, value 8s., the goods of Ralph Wilcoxon.
RALPH WILCOXON. I live in Walker's-court, St. James', and am a shoemaker. I was called into my shop on the 29th of October, and saw two men walking out; I followed Munro, who was one of them, and said I suspected he had a pair of shoes in his hat - I asked him to let me look at it; he said, No, I should not: I sent for an officer - he then came back to my shop, pulled a pair of shoes from his hat, and ran away; I followed, and brought him back - the officer then arrived; another pair of shoes fell from some part of his person into the street.
THOMAS GOOK. I went to the place, and took Munro - there was a scuffle between them, and a pair of shoes fell from his left arm.
JAMES EADE. I am in the prosecutor's employ. On the 29th of October I was fitting Morgan with a pair of shoes; Munro took a pair and put them under his coat -I called my master; Morgan put on his old shoes, and walked away with Munro - as he got to the door he said"It is all right Jack."
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you ever seen Morgan before? A. No; he did not seem to be assisting the other man in taking them - he did not appear intoxicated.
WILLIAM GREEN. On the morning after the robbery I went with Eade to the Quadrant, in Regent-street, and presently Morgan came out; I took him to the watch-house - he owned a cap which Munro had on, the night before; I had assisted in taking Munro.
Prisoner MORGAN. If I was in the shop it was unknown to me - I was very much intoxicated.
MUNRO - GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Six Months.
MORGAN - GUILTY. Aged 36.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18281204-231]
231. WILLIAM EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of November, 1 gown, value 6s.; 1 half-crown, and 2 shillings, the property of Sarah Harman.
SARAH HARMAN. I am single. On the 20th of November I asked the prisoner to go and shew me the way to a pawnbroker's, at Pimlico, where my gown was in pawn; we got it out, and he took it under his arm; I asked him for it - he said he would carry it; when we came to Tothill-street we went to Mr. Jones', to get a ring out of pawn - I asked the prisoner to read the duplicate, which he did, and gave it to the pawnbroker; I laid my handkerchief, with the gown in it, on the counter and pulled out half a crown and two shillings - the prisoner took them up, and said he should be back in a minute or two, and went out.
SAMUEL SMITH. I took this gown in pawn of the prisoner on the 20th of November, at Mr. Archibald's, Bridge-road, Lambeth.
WILLIAM IVEING. I apprehended the prisoner in Lower Grosvenor-place, on the 5th instant - he said it was all made up; that he had sent the duplicate and 2s.
SARAH HARRISON. The affidavit was sent to me by a young man, but I would not take it - he threw it down on a table in a public-house.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in liquor.
GUILTY. Aged 33.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-232]
232. SARAH QUIN was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of November, 2 half-crowns, and 2 shillings, the monies of George Hancock, from his person.
GEORGE HANCOCK. I am a smith. I was at the White Lion public-house, in Oxford-street; I took the prisoner there, and gave her a glass of gin; we did not sit down at all - she put her hand into my right - hand trousers-pocket, and took out my money which was loose; she ran away -I pursued and took her in two minutes, in Berner's-street- the officer found the money.
THOMAS FULLER. I took the prisoner, in consequence of what the prosecutor said - she was running down Berners-street, and the prosecutor after her: I told her the prosecutor said she had robbed him; she said she had not; Hancock said she had the two half-crown pieces in her hand- I went to take hold of her hand - she clenched it, and said she had no money of her own, or any body else's; he said she should go to the watch-house if she did not give them up; she would not, and we went to the watch-house; there was a charge there, and we had to sit down and wait - she dropped one half-crown; I did not see it, but a person unknown picked it up, and said it fell from her, but she denied it - the person threw the half-crown on the watch-house table - the prisoner said it was hers, and she would have it - she then threw down the other half-crown, and then took them both up; the watch-house keeper said "I insist upon your returning that;" she began to make a great noise, and said she would not; I held her hands while the watchman took her pocket, shook it over the table, and shook out two shillings - the prosecutor said that was the rest of his money - he had before said he lost 7s.
ANTHONY READ. I was constable of the night. The prisoner was brought in - the charges were rather numerous, and they were obliged to wait in the lobby; I was told the prisoner had dropped one half-crown; when they were brought in, the prosecutor said very distinctly that she had taken two half-crowns and two shillings - one half-crown was brought in by an individual from the lobby, who said it had dropped form her hand or her person; it was laid on the table; the prisoner threw down the other, and then said"It is my money - I have earned it," or some rather low expression, and she would have it; the watch-house-keeper said she was not to have it; she seized it with her hand - it was taken from her, and her pocket; she resisted a good deal, but when she saw we were determined to search, she appeared as if she would strip - we took from her pocket two shillings and a sixpence; the prosecutor had before described what he had lost; he did not claim the sixpence, and we gave it her for refreshment; the prosecutor was rather in liquor, and said before the Magistrate that it was two half-crowns, one shilling, and two sixpences; that is the reason of my being here.
Prisoner's Defence. When I met the prosecutor, he was taking to two women, and he said to me "Old girl, will you have a drop of gin - do you know where there is a house open?" I said "The Waterman's Arms public-house is always open;" there were four or five people in the house; I went out, he came after me, and said if I would go up a dark passage, he would give me 1s., and I refused - he swore, and said what did he give me the glass of gin for; he said "Wh-s could get as much in one night as he could in a week;" I pulled out part of my own money from my pocket, and he said "I will swear to the watchman you robbed me, if you don't go with me;" 7s. 6d. was taken from me, and they gave me the sixpence back; he said for a long time that I took one shilling and two sixpences, the money I had was my own; I met a gentleman who gave it to me; the prosecutor tore the collar off my gown, when he tried to get the money - he took hold of my neck, and wanted to take liberties with me; it was the watchman who got us into the public-house; he said if we would be quiet, be would get us in; I think the prosecutor and the watchman know each other, because they both said if I would give up the money, they would say nothing about it.
THOMAS FULLER. The landlord called me to the house to turn some disorderly persons out, and while I was at thedoor this happened; I live next door to the prosecutor almost; it was between twelve and one o'clock.
GUILTY. Aged 33.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-233]
233. MARGARET BAYLAN was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 watch, value 30s.; 1 watch-key, value 5s.; 2 seals, value 15s.; 1 bag, value 1d.; 1 shilling, and 1 crown, the property of John Holland, from his person.
JOHN HOLLAND. On the 27th of November, I met with eight or ten friends - we dined together; I took rather too much wine; I had to go home, about a mile from town, and between ten and eleven o'clock, I was so unwell that I was glad to sit down, in Penton-street, before Mr. Selcock's, where there are seats and a table before the door; the window and doors were open, and an oyster-man had got his oysters on the table; a woman approached me, and unhappily I went into the settle with her; I had not been there more than a minute and a half, before I lost my purse and watch; the purse contained four sovereigns, a Queen Elizabeth's shilling, of 1566, a crown-piece of Charles the Second, and some few shillings; there was a key and two seals to my watch; they were all taken from me, but I know no more about it than a child unborn; I did not discover the loss till I got to my own door, in White Conduit-fields- I have seen some of them since.
THOMAS BOWSTEAD. On the 27th of November, between eleven and half-past eleven o'clock, I apprehended the prisoner, in St. John's-street-road; she threw down this purse in the watch-house, and the watch-house-keeper took this watch from her bosom, in my presence; I heard a sovereign slip from her - and on the road to the watch-house a sovereign fell from her, which my fellow-watchman took up; a dirty white pocket-handkerchief was found on her, with a bad shilling in the corner of it.
JAMES CATWALL. I assisted in taking the prisoner; she pulled her hand from me several times; I then secured her as fast as I could; she tried several times to shake something from her - one sovereign fell from her, and I picked it up; she was about three hundred yards from Mr. Selcock's when she was taken, and appeared coming from there.
JOHN WIGGINS. I was constable of the night: the prisoner was brought in, and these articles were found - she threw down the purse or bag on one of the benches - I took it up; I found this watch on the right-side of her bosom -I then shook her gown, and a sovereign dropped from her person; I told her I supposed she had been robbing a gentleman - she said nothing.
JAMES HOLLAND. This is my watch, and this is my bag, and this shilling; I had them in my possession between ten and eleven o'clock; I was, comparatively speaking, insensible.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming along the City-road- I saw some paper, kicked it, and found it wet; I took it up - there was nothing but the watch and the shilling in it; I went on, met a young woman, and we went into the Clown public-house: I had been with my friend, who knew me when in service, and he gave me 2l.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-234]
234. DENNIS BRIANT was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of December, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Charles Marten, from his person.
CHARLES MARTEN. On Friday last, I was going through Whitechapel, on the Plaistow stage; I felt a little twitch at my pocket - I turned, and saw the prisoner dropping from the coach, with my handkerchief; I called to the man to stop, and jumped down - the prisoner turned down Great Garden-street, and ran down several streets; I pursued, and took him - I only lost sight of him as he turned the corner; he had dropped the handkerchief - I brought him back, and some person had picked up the handkerchief, in the line which he had run; from the observation I had of him at the back of the coach, I should be doubtful of his person, but I scarcely lost sight of him till I took him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMAS SMITH. I produce the handkerchief. Two men brought the prisoner to the watch-house; the prosecutor said, in his presence, that he never lost sight of him from the time he dropped from the coach, with the handkerchief in his hand.
CHARLES MARTEN re-examined. It was not above a minute or two before I took him; he was walking then - I had run him out of breath; I should not have the smallest hesitation in saying he is the person.
Prisoner's Defence. I was turning round the corner, and he collared me.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-235]
235. JACOB LOVELL was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of December, 3 cwt. of lead, value 3l., the goods of William Marshall.
WILLIAM MARSHALL. I am a builder, and also a victualler, at Stepney. On Wednesday evening, the 3d of December, I had 3cwt. of lead rolled up in my back premises; I had seen it before dusk in the evening, and next morning I missed it - the prisoner lives in the same street as I do, and was at my house, playing at skittles, with several others, that evening; they went away early.
THOMAS DYKE. I am a green-grocer. I saw the prisoner and another person, on Wednesday evening, between seven and eight o'clock; he said he wanted to hire a truck, to go to Spitalfields-market, to get some greens, at half-past five o'clock, the next morning; I asked where he lived, and I believe he said he lived in Westover-street, and the other in Victory-row; the prisoner deposited 1s., and went away; next morning, between five and six o'clock, the prisoner came, according to my directions - I went down, unlocked two trucks, and he said, "Let me have the light one;" I said, "Very well - and now, my lad, you say you are going to Spitalfields-market;" he said,"I am" - I said, "Have the goodness to tell some person to look after it;" he said, "There are two of us going, and I will take care of it" - he went off, and did not turn to the left, but to the right; I shut my door, and followed him - he went down the Bow-road, turned down a street, and then down another street, which led to Westover-street; he then turned again, and went into the street where the lead was lying, as I suppose, but I did not see it; I expected he would come to the top of that street - I went round to meet him, but did not see him; I listened, andheard the truck in another street - I went there, and saw him dragging it, and the other pushing behind; they went across the Mile-end-road, and down Globe-road- I stopped them, and said, "What are you about, going up and down streets in this manner?" the man behind went away, and the prisoner said he had been after his mate, or partner, I do not know which; I saw a piece of carpet, or sack, in the truck, and five pieces of lead - I said, "This is thieving, and I must take you to the watch-house;" he said, "The other is gone, do let me go" I said, "No, to the watch-house you must go;" a man was coming by in a blue jacket and white apron - I asked him to assist; he said he should not, and went away - I asked the prisoner to assist in shoving up to the watch-house; he went about twenty yards, and then ran away - I cried Stop thief! but he ran by the watchman, and I saw no more of him till between six and seven o'clock in the evening; I got the lead to the watch-house.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You did not see whether the young man gave the prisoner the lead? A. No.
JAMES TOGG. I saw the prisoner come, on Wednesday night, to hire the truck; I had known him as pot-boy at the Three Crowns public-house, Mile-end-road.
JOHN BROWN. I saw the lead at the watch-house; to the best of my knowledge, it was the same I had worked some of for Mr. Marshall - there are no marks on it.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it fixed? A. No - it was lying in the skittle-ground; if I saw it at a distance, I could not swear to it.
ROBERT CHRISTIAN. I saw the lead brought to the watch-house; I showed it to Brown, and to Mr. Marshall.
GEORGE HENFREY. I took the prisoner from the skittle-ground, at Mr. Marshall's, the same evening; Marshall told me to come down, between six and seven o'clock- I went, and saw the prisoner; Mr. Dyke said he was one he saw with the truck - I charged him with it; he said he knew nothing about it - Mr. Dyke said he was certain he was one; he said he was at home, and in bed, at the time.
Prisoner's Defence. I was at home, and in my bed; I did not get up till eight o'clock - I went to the skittleground in the evening, and then went home; I went there again, and was taken.
MARY ANN LOVELL. I am the prisoner's sister-in-law- I married his brother. On Thursday morning last, he was in bed from six to eight o'clock; I live in the same house with him.
COURT. Q. How can you swear to that? A.Because I was up with my baby, who was ill; I went down, to put some water into the kettle, between five and six o'clock, and saw him in bed - I passed the bed, and could not help seeing him.
MARY LOVELL. I am the prisoner's mother. On the night before he had given his trousers to me to mend, and I found them where I left them, after eight o'clock in the morning - he had but that one pair to wear; I sleep in the back-room; he could not get out without my knowing it.
ALICE LOVELL. I am the prisoner's sister. He was at home, and in bed, from six to eight o'clock last Thursday morning; I am a dress-maker - I got up at six o'clock to work; I went for a candle, and he was in bed, and appeared to be sound asleep - my sister was up at the same time.
ELIZABETH LOVELL. I am the prisoner's sister. Last Thursday morning, my sister and I got up at six o'clock, to work; I went into the kitchen, to wash my hands, about five minutes past six o'clock - the prisoner was then asleep.
JOSEPH LOVELL. I am the prisoner's brother - I sleep up stairs; I got up about five o'clock, and struck a light, as the child was crying - my brother was then in bed, and the doors had not been opened; I went into the kitchen between five and six o'clock, and the doors were fastened as I left them - I had occasion to go to the yard.
COURT. Q.What doors are there to the house? A. The kitchen-door and the street-door; the key of the street-door is generally in the lock when we go to bed.
GEORGE GOOD. I live with Mr. Marshall; my mistress said there were two suspicious characters come to the house; I went to inquire on Stepney-green, if they lived there, and they did - I went to Mr. Dyke, and he said he could not exactly swear to Lovell, if it had not been for Togg's boy.
THOMAS DYKE re-examined. Q. Did you say you should not have known him. but for Togg's boy? A. No; Good came to me and said "Have you not made a mistake in the person you have sworn to?" I said No; he said"Mrs. Marshall says there are two very suspicious lads in the skittle-ground, and she would be glad if you would come and look at them;" I said I should not; I was quite certain of his person - he was between two very large gas lamps that morning. and I had him in my grasp five minutes; if I were going to die I could take my oath of it.
ROBERT CHRISTIAN re-examined. Q.Were you before the Magistrate? A. Yes; and Marshall, Dyke, Togg and Brown - Good was not there; I know him well - I am afraid he is a dangerous boy from his general character; I would not believe him on his oath.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q.Why would you not believe him? A.Because I have known him tell great untruths; I never knew him on his oath before - but Mrs. Marshall says she never sent him at all - it is from what she said that I state what I have on this occasion; I have had him once or twice at the watch-house for little rows, and there he proved himself a great story-teller, but I cannot charge my memory with one.
MR. MARSHALL re-examined. Good is in my employ; I have a very bad opinion of him - if any loose characters come there he is always with them; I was going to discharge him on Saturday.
JURY. Q. Did your wife send him to Dyke, to come and look at the persons in the ground? A. No, she did not.
NOT GUILTY.
