230. WILLIAM MAXWELL CAMPAIGN was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of November, 18 sovereigns, the monies of George Middleton, in the dwelling-house of Benjamin Griffith .
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution.
GEORGE MIDDLETON. I am in the service of Mr. Griffith, a butcher, of Hampstead, and slept in the third-floor - the prisoner lived in the same service, and slept in the room over mine. On the 23d of November, on going to my box, I found it locked as usual, but missed eighteen sovereigns from it - I had no mark on them.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long has he lived there? A. One year and a quarter - three persons slept in my room.
BENJAMIN GRIFFITH. The prisoner and prosecutor were in my service; the prisoner had 4s. a week - I was present when he was searched, and 6l. 16s. was found on his person, and in his box together - he said his friends had sent it to him.
Cross-examined. Q. Is he not the son of respectable parents? A. He is.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-237]
237. WILLIAM MORLEY and THOMAS OUSBY were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Carpenter Austin, on the 27th of October, at St. Leonard, Shoreditch, and stealing therein 3 spoons, value 15s.; 1 pair of shoes, value 6s.; 1 key, value 6d.; 1 poker, value 6d., an 18d., in copper monies, his property.
SECOND COUNT. stating it to be the dwelling-house of Richard Austin and the said George Carpenter Austin.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the prosecution.
GEORGE CARPENTER AUSTIN. I am in partnership with my father Richard Austin - we are type-founders, and live at No. 10 and 11, Worship-street, Shoreditch - a servant sleeps at No. 10 and I sleep at No. 11, and have that house to myself; the taxes are paid out of the partnership funds - there is no communication from No. 11 to No. 10, - there is a yard behind the two houses, and that yard is made into one; there was a partition between them, which has been taken down. On the 26th of October, about two o'clock in the morning, I heard a noise at my bed-room door; I sleep in the front-room, first-floor - the noise awoke me; I got up, looked towards the door, and saw a light through the key-hole - I called out Halloo! I heard a loud voice, and immediately heard persons running down stairs; I ran to my window, looked into the street, and saw three men come out, one after the other from the house-door of No. 10, as it appeared from the direction - they ran towards the turnpike; I sprang a rattle immediately, and lost sight of them - some watch-men came up when I was at the window, and my boy had just then run down stairs from No. 10, and got to the door: I went down stairs to the back-door, and found it open - the watchmen came in; I went with them into the kitchen of No. 11, examined the window, and found about half a pane of glass cut out: a man must have put his arm through there, and taken out an iron fastening - that would enable him to throw the window up; they appeared to have got in that way - the iron was fastened at night; it was afterwards found out in the yard: I have no doubt they had entered there. From the drawers in that kitchen I missed a silver tea-spoon and table-spoon; I missed all the property stated in the indictment, and among other things, a pair of shoes - I went to the watch-house, and found the prisoners there; I knew Morley before - he had lived a considerable time in my service, and left about two years ago- the tea-spoons were produced to me there; they were those I had missed from my kitchen; I saw the key of the street-door of No. 10, at the watch-house, and the kitchen poker. The house is in Shoreditch parish.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. It appeared to you they came from No. 10, you cannot tell what door they came from? A. No; I was looking out of window and could not see the door - I could not distinguish the men; at the time - it was too dark to distinguish Morley; the was down and the window was very dirty.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You found the back-door of No. 11 open, and the key gone? A. Yes; I found the backdoor of No. 10 open when I came back.
WILLIAM EDMONDS. I am a watchman - my box faces the toll-bar in Worship-street. On the morning of the 27th of October, about a quarter, or twenty minutes past two, I heard a rattle spring; I ran into the road, and saw three persons running about fifty yards off, coming in a direction across the road, from Mr. Austin's house, and about twenty yards from the house, or not quite so much - I made the best of my way after them, and saw Mr. Austin's head out of window: in consequence of what he said I went after them round the new market, and turning the corner, I saw Kemp with Ousby in custody; I assisted in taking him to the watch-house - I cannot say whether he was one of the three I had seen running; as we were taking him to the watch-house, I felt something fall against my feet - it jingled on the ground; it was dark - there was nobody but we three, and Nixon, another watchman; I stooped down and picked up a spoon, which I gave to Berry, the officer - I afterwards went back and examined the house.
Prisoner OUSBY. Q. At the time I was going to the watch-house you had hold of one of my arms, and Kemp the other? A. Yes; I will not swear you dropped the spoon - I kicked it myself; I had seen three persons running before you were caught.
GEORGE KEMP. I am a watchman. On the 27th of October, about ten minutes past two o'clock, I was in North-street, Finsbury-market; I heard a rattle spring, went towards the sound, and saw three men running towards the market - I followed them and tripped up Ousby, who was one of them, and took him; Morley got away, but I am certain he was one of them - I took Ousby into custody, and as we were taking him to the watch-house, I saw him put his right hand to his coat-pocket; I afterwards heard something fall to the ground, and the spoon was picked up - I had also seen him at work at his breeches-pocket; it dropped from him - I searched him at the watch-house, and found on him a key, a dark lantern, and 10d. or 1s. in copper; Morley was brought to me in the market, by Lovett, and on him I found 2 1/2d. - I knew him to be one of the men I had seen running; I afterwards asked Ousby where the money was, which the houseman had given him back - he said he had thrown it down the privy.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it not a very dark morning? A. It was.
JOHN DIXON. I am a watchman. I know Austin's house - it is in the parish of St. Leonard, Shoreditch; I heard a rattle spring, ran up, and found Ousby in custody, and in the market-place I took a shoe from his person.
JONATHAN LOVETT. I am a watchman. On the 27th of October, about half-past two o'clock in the morning, I was in Long-alley, and in consequence of what I heard, I went to Crown-street and Clifton-street; I saw Morley coming from the new market, down Clifton-street, at a smartish pace; I cannot say he was running, nor yet walking - he was sixty or seventy yards from Austin's house - I took hold of him; he said "What do you want with me? What have I done?" I said "I don't know, you know best, you must come back with me" he said he should not - I took him back, and found Kemp taking Ousby along; I said "Kemp, do you want this man?" he said Yes - he identified him directly; I searched him, and found 2 1/2d. on him.
RICHARD HATCH. I am a watchman. I was with Lovett, heard a rattle spring, went with him, and saw Morley going at a sharp pace; I saw him taken - Kemp identified him immediately.
THOMAS BERRY. I am a headborough of Finsbury. -I was present when the prisoners were brought to the watch-house; we searched them - I produce the property found on Ousby.
GEORGE KEMP. These two spoons were given to me by Edmonds and Dixon - they were found in the street; I found a key on Ousby.
GEORGE CARPENTER AUSTIN. This is the key of No. 10, and was safe in the house overnight; these are the spoons, and I believe these shoes to be mine, but do not swear to them - this is my poker.
Cross-examined. Q.How long had Morley lived with you? A. More than two years; I had no character with him - he left me of his own accord.
MORLEY'S Defence. I know nothing about it; I was taken accidentally walking along, and did not know what for - the other prisoner is quite a stranger to me; another young man was apprehended in the street, just in the same way, and he was liberated before the Magistrate.
GEORGE KEMP. I saw three men running - I had them before me for some time; I am certain Morley was one of them.
OUSBY'S Defence. I know nothing of the other prisoner.
MORLEY - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 21.
OUSBY - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 20.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury, on account of their youth.
[trialAccount t18281204-238]
Before Mr. Recorder.
238. JOHN LOVELL, THOMAS EYRES and EMILY COX were indicted for stealing, on the 22d of November, at St. John, Hampstead, 1 sheep, price 50s., the property of Thomas Billen and William Billen.
SECOND COUNT, for killing the said sheep, with intent to steal the carcass; against the Statute.
THOMAS BILLEN. I am a drover, in partnership with my brother William Billen. I had eighty-six sheep in a field in Hampstead parish - they were all right last Thursday fortnight; I saw them all there about the middle of the day - I did not see them again till the Saturday morning; I did not then count them, but on the Sunday morning I missed this one - it was marked J. W. on the near hip, and ruddled between the shoulders; I have seen the skin since - I saw it in the field that morning; the sheep had been flayed in the field, and the carcass taken away - I am sure it is the skin of my sheep; the prisoners were quite strangers to me - the sheep was worth about 2l. 10s.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you any other partner? A. No.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I am a Police-officer of Mary-le-bone. On Saturday, the 22d of November, at a quarter-past seven o'clock in the morning, I was standing in Lisson-grove, at the end of Alpha-row, and saw Cox coming towards me - she had got a long grey cloak on; I saw her holding something very heavy under her cloak - I went up and asked what she had got there; she turned back her cloak, I opened a basket which she had got, and in it I found a leg and shoulder of mutton - it did not appear to have been cut by a butcher: I asked how she accounted for having it - she said a man had given it to her up the grove; I asked her if she knew who the man was- she said she never saw him before in her life; this was about a mile and a half, or three quarters from the field at Hampstead, where the sheep were kept. I asked her again if she did not know the man who gave it to her; she said No, she did not - I then asked her how she came there so early; she said she was going to Kilburn to work- I asked if she knew the party's name who she was going to work for; she said she did not - I asked if she had ever been there before - she said No, but a person had recommended her; I then inquired where she was taking the mutton to - she replied "Down this way," (meaning down the grove) I asked where to - she declined saying, and begged she might have her child before she went to prison; I asked where her child was - she said at her father and mother's where she lived: I asked her where it was - she said towards the Edgware-road, and she took me to the Junction-road; I asked her where she lived - she said next door to the baker's; I left her in charge of Wall and others, while I went to inquire, and at last found the child at No. 20. Junction-road, where she lived: I found the room-door locked - I returned back, and asked her for the key of her room-door; she gave it to me and said "I will take you where there is more, if you will go with me;" I went; unlocked the room-door with the key, and the first thing I found was a sheep's heart, a quantity of mutton fat in a dish, and in a handkerchief I found a quantity of mutton tied up, that did not appear to have been cut by a butcher; I also found a quantity of mutton in a brown pan - I left Wall in the house, and returned to Cox; she went with us to No. 2, Devonshire-street, Lisson-grove, where Lovell lived - I went into the second-floor front-room; Lovell was there with his wife - he got up and came towards me; I said, "I believe your name is Lovell - he said, "Yes, it is; I know nothing at all of the meat whatever, how it came here" - I then looked round, and on a chest of drawers I found a quantity of mutton that had not been cut by a butcher; there was a loin, breast, and something, it was cut so awkward I could not tell the joints, but there was nearly half a sheep; I found a leg of mutton in a flat rush basket, on the chest of drawers - that did not appear to have been cut by a butcher; he then turned his head towards me and said, in rather a soft voice, "Webster don't make the worst of this," (I suppose he knew me before) - he said, "You are not obliged to say you have seen it;" I said, "Yes, I am, as an officer I am bound to say it" - I then said, "Don't say any thing to hurt yourself, for I am bound to report every word you say in evidence;" he said no more - I then found in a chair, under the window, a pair of corded breeches, which were stained with blood; it appeared fresh - I said, "These are bloody, I must take them;" I then looked on the right side of the bed, and found this white navigator's jacket, also smeared with blood about the sleeves - it appeared of the same freshness as that on the breeches; we then tied the meat and clothes up and took them to the office, with Lovell and Cox-Eyres was taken about an hour afterwards, but I was not present; I saw the skin found in the field, and at the office saw the meat tried to the skin - in my judgment it certainly fitted it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was an entire sheep found? A. I think almost the whole of it, but I cannot say, from the way it was cut - the whole sheep was not fitted to the skin, but a joint here and there; it certainly corresponded as I thought; Lovell lives about a mile and three-quarters from the field.
WILLIAM HENRY HEWITT. I went with Webster and Cox to where we found Lovell - his evidence is correct; I observed all he has stated.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. I am an officer. I apprehended Eyres on the Saturday morning - after Webster and Hewitt were gone, I waited in the front-room secondfloor of the house where Cox lodged in the back-room, and about a quarter-past nine o'clock I saw Eyres go to the door of Cox's room - he tried the door, but could not get in; I had the key in my pocket - I went out, and at the door found a female speaking to Eyres; I said to her, "Is this Cox," (meaning Eyres) - she said, "No, it is not, it is Smith;" Eyres was going away from the house towards Edgware-road; I walked by his side, and saw there was a great deal of wool on his waistcoat, and his shoes were all over fresh clay - I went up to him, and said, "Smith, are you out of work;" he said he was - I then said, "As you have been inquiring after Cox I shall expect you will go back with me to the house;" he said, "I may as well go back and sit with you as any body else" - we went into the front-room; I had some suspicion of him and searched him there, and in his right-hand jacket-pocket I found this knife, all covered with fresh blood, and very greasy, as if it had been cutting fat meat, and in his right-hand waistcoat-pocket I found this cord wet, and covered with wool; there was no blood on it - I then told him I should detain him on suspicion of being concerned with Cox; he made no reply - I did not tell him for what; I waited there about an hour or more, expecting Cox's husband would come in, but he did no:- she is a married woman; I sent to Paddington watch-house for a constable to stop in the room, while I took Eyres to the office - I was present on the Sunday morning, with Webster and another young man, at Hampstead, where the skin was found; I saw the meat applied to the skin and it tallied - there were holes in the skin, which corresponded with the meat.
JAMES BEATTIE. I work for my father, who is a navigator, employed at Mr. Wright's, at Hampstead; we live about a quarter of a mile from this field. I was walking round Baker's field, where the sheep are kept, on Saturday, the 22d of November, about twelve o'clock in the morning, and saw a skin lying in the ditch; I went up to Mr. Baker and told him of it - I afterwards saw some mutton applied to the skin, and in my judgment it had come from that skin; I know the prisoners - I had not seen them about there; Eyres and Lovell worked for my father at Hampstead, but were not at work for a long time before this.
JOSEPH TUTNER. I am a private watchman. About ten o'clock on the Friday night the two male prisoners went by me with another man - I knew their persons before; my station is on the Edgware-road - I do not know Baker's field; I was about two miles from Hampstead town - I wished them good night; one of them said, "I know you, old man;" I said, "Yes, and I know you."
Cross-examined. Q. You were at least two miles from Hampstead-church? A. I cannot say.
WILLIAM BAKER. I am a farmer, and owner of this field - it is about a mile and half from Tutner's beat. I went with Billen to Mary-le-bone-office on the Monday as the sheep was stolen out of my field - I saw the skin at the office, and some of the meat applied to it, but did not take sufficient notice to see if it fitted; Lovell had mowed for me for three or four years, and Eyres I have known twelve years.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you had a good opinion of him? A. Yes. There was a good deal of meat at the office; I saw them apply part of the loins - it was on the Monday.
Q. How many thousand sheep would this skin have fitted? Q. I cannot say: it was a common sized sheep.
THOMAS KING. I am a butcher. I saw a skin, which Billen claimed, and saw some meat at the office on the Monday; I saw it applied to the skin - it was not cut as a butcher would cut it; I should think nearly the whole carcass was produced at the office - there were about twenty pieces, but it was not in joints; I saw one piece fitted - the head was taken off at the second joint, and the head left behind in the skin; I applied part of the neck to the head - it corresponded, and other parts, in my judgment, agreed with it; in my opinion, it was the meat that came from that skin; I have been a butcher from a child - my father was one.
Cross-examined. Q. The necks of sheep are generally about a size? A. Yes.
Q. It might have fitted hundreds of the same sized sheep? A. That is rather doubtful - it was cut in a particular manner; it appeared to have been killed within a day or two - it could not have been killed four or five days from the appearance of it; it is possible a man might be mistaken, but there are a great many people of the same opinion as myself - there are not many sheep of that size; it is a very large one - I will not swear there are not a thousand; it is possible to be mistaken as to the time it had been killed.
COURT. Q.Did you ever see a sheep killed in that way before? A. No.
SAMUEL RENSHAW. I am a butcher. I saw the sheepskin and the meat at the office on the Saturday; it did not appear to have been cut by a butcher - I saw it applied to the skin; I have been a butcher about three years, and in my judgment the meat fitted the skin; I saw the neck and loin compared - they corresponded with the marks in the skin, and, in my opinion, belonged to the same skin; I never saw a sheep slaughtered in that way before.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not mean to swear they belonged to that skin? A. No.
COURT. Q.Would it have fitted the skin of any sheep slaughtered by a butcher in the regular way? A. NO.
ISAAC ANNS. I am a publican, and live in Earl-street, Lisson-grove. The two male prisoners came into my house, with a man named Cox, who has absconded - they were there about two o'clock on the Friday afternoon; I served them with two pots of porter; they staid about half an hour, drank their beer, and said they would pay for it when they got into work, and I was satisfied.
LOVELL'S Defence. I leave it to my Counsel.
Seven witnesses gave both the male prlsoners a good character.
LOVELL - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 25.
EYRES - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 42.
Recommended to Mercy by the prosecutor, believing it to be their first offence; and by the Jury, on account of their character.
COX - NOT GUILTY.

239. WILLIAM AYRES and RICHARD KENNEY were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 4 coats, value 7l; 4 waistcoats, value 12s; 1 pair of breeches, value 5s.; 1 pair of boots, value 5s.; 1 hat, value 15s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 6d.; 1 pair of shoes, value 5s., and 1 piece of baize, value 1s., the goods of William Davison, in his dwelling-house.
WILLIAM DAVISON. I am a coachman, and live at No. 3, Little Grove-street, Lisson-grove, St. Mary-le-bone - it is stables: I sleep there occasionally, and have a bed there constantly - I keep part of my clothes there. On the 25th of October I lost some property out of the stables - I had seen the stables about nine o'clock in the morning, before the things were stolen; I cannot say whether they were locked - I was at the stables again in the evening, about seven, but did not go up stairs: the room I sleep in is not generally locked - I had seen the clothes a day or two before: I found the prisoners in custody on Monday morning, the 27th of October, and some clothes were produced which I thought to be mine - I had bought them old; I saw some coats, but cannot say whether there were four -I believe they were mine; I have a doubt of it.
Q. Had some coats been taken from your room between the morning of the 25th of October and the time you saw them at the office? A. I should not like to swear that - I could not swear it: it was dark when I got up; I cannot swear I left any clothes there.
Q. Were they there the night before, when you went to bed? A. There were very few things there when I went up at night; I lost things from the stable, because the things I had seen there and worn before were not there.
Q. Well, I ask you whether the things you had there were gone in the morning? A. Yes - I did not understand you; I lost a box-coat, and saw a box-coat at the office - it did not look so fresh and so well as mine, when I saw it last; I thought it was mine, and swore it was mine to the best of my knowledge - I might have had it four months; it was of the same colour: it was very large, and would fit almost two of my size - it was very much tumbled about- I thought it was mine; I should not like to swear to it: I saw some breeches at the office, and supposed them to be mine - I had had them about the place for a week; I should be sorry to swear to them: I thought the boots mine, but cannot swear to them - there was a hat, which I believe to be mine, but there is no particular mark on it; there was a pair of new shoes - I had never worn them; they had no mark on them - I never fitted them on; I lost a pair, but should not like to swear to them - a piece of baize was produced; I suppose it to be mine, but should not like to swear to it.
Cross-examined by MR. SMITH. Q. Are you sure your name is not Davis? A. Yes; I have passed by the name of Davis when I was in a gentleman's service, but it is Davison.
WILLIAM WESTBY. I am a watchman. On the 25th of October, about eleven o'clock at night, I was on my beat in Princes-street, Mary-le bone, about one hundred yards from these stables, and saw three men going along Princes-street; one of them was some distance in front of the other two - one of them was Ayres: I suspected they had something, as Ayres and another had bundles on their backs - when they had got about one hundred yards I went after them; they were walking very fast - I came up to them; they had just put their bundles down: I asked Ayres what he had got there - he said, "It is nothing to you, I dare say;" I said, "If it don't belong to me, most likely it belongs to some person - I should like to look at it;" he said there it was, I might look at it if I liked: I stooped to examine it, when he and the other man ran away - I sprang my rattle, called Stop theif! and Ayres was stopped; I saw him in five minutes, and was certain of him - the bundle he had contained a box-coat, three body coats, four waistcoats, a pair of breeches, a pair of drawers, a white neck-cloth, a pair of boots and shoes, a hat, and a piece of green baize; he was taken to Mary-le-bone office, and the things were produced: I saw Davison there on the Monday - Ayres told me at the watch-house that he was employed by two men, named David Evans and Richard Kenney, to carry these things, and they were to give him 6d. for his trouble; Kenney was taken the same night, for assaulting a watchman - I could not identify him as the man who dropped the other bundle, for he hung down his head; what he dropped was a box-coat; it was taken to the office, and seen by Davison.
JOSEPH STOWLEY. I am a baker. At a quarter-past eleven o'clock on Saturday night I stood at my shop-door, which is about two hundred yards from this stable, and saw the prisoners pass by me, in company - I am quite certain of them; Kenney was carrying something, which appeared to be a box-coat, over his shoulder - the watchman passed me just then; I saw Kenney in custody on Monday, and was quite certain of him - I did not a third man with them: I called the watchman directly they passed me, and he questioned them; Ayres ran away; and came just by me; the watchman sprang his rattle - I pursued them both; Kenney turned round Salisbury-street, and escaped - Ayres pulled off a hat, and threw it into a pond of water; the place is not a thoroughfare - he turned back, and I took him: I am quite certain of him.
Cross-examined. Q. It was a quarter-past eleven o'clock? A. Yes, but it was a full moon-light night; I never saw him before, to my knowledge.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. I am a constable. On Sunday morning, the 26th of October, I went with Davison to No. 10, Nightingale-street, Lisson-grove, as Davisonsaid he was robbed the overnight, and suspected Evans,(who had been in his service) was concerned; I took Evans - I found Kenney at the watch-house: he had been taken overnight - Evans saw both the prisoners at the watch-house; Ayres said, in the presence of both of them, that they were the persons who had employed him to carry the things - I said to Kenney, "Then I shall detain you;" he said nothing to it; he neither denied nor admitted it.
Q. Ayres gave this account the moment you asked him? A. Yes; I took the two men by his description; Kenney was brought in on another charge.
The prosecutor being again examined, looked singly at the articles produced, and refused to swear to any one of them.
COURT. (Shewing him his deposition.) - Q. Look at this name of William Davison, is that your writing? A. Yes, I believe it is - I cannot say whether it was read over to me before I signed it; I was so frightened, so flurried, I did not know what I was doing; to the best of my knowledge what I swore was true.
Q. You have sworn "The box-coat, three other coats, four waistcoats, a pair of kerseymere breeches, and other articles now produced by William Westby, are my property, and part of the articles stolen from my room;" that is what you have sworn - now you cannot swear to any one of them? A. I might say so; I beg pardon; I was so much frightened at the time, and the things were so tumbled about.
NOT GUILTY.
The prosecutor was committed to Newgate.
[trialAccount t18281204-240]
240. GEORGE CONSTANCE was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Hewit, on the 2d of October, and stealing one cribbage-board, value 10s.; 5 books, value 1l.; 1 shawl, value 16s.; 3 napkins, value 3s.; 2 bed-gowns, value 1s., and 1 shirt, value 6d., the goods of George Frost.
ELIZABETH FROST. I am the wife of George Frost, and lodge in Perry-street, Somers'-town, on the second-floor - Thomas Hewit, the landlord, lives in the house; two young men lived in the back-room, first-floor; two parlours were occupied by other lodgers; I went out about half-past five o'clock in the afternoon of the 2d of October, I locked the door I am quite positive, and left all the property safe in the room; I returned about one o'clock with my husband, and found the door wide open; the lock was half back, it was half out of the box - it appeared as if it had been picked with a false key; I missed the articles stated in the indictment, and saw all of those at Mary-le-bone-office on or about the 20th of October, but I missed more - the prisoner was in custody; he was one of the lodgers in the house, but we were not intimate with him at all, and never visited; he lodged in the back-room first-floor - he called himself a piano-forte maker, out of employ; I have a letter which I received - I do not know the hand-writing - it came by post on Monday.
RICHARD FAIRLAM. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Lisson-grove. I have a shawl which I took in pawn for 8s. on the 3d of October from a female, in the name of Mary Bond; it was claimed by Mrs. Frost at the office.
WALTER FAIRLAM. I am servant to Mr. Gideon, a pawnbroker, of Stafford-street, Lisson-green. I have a sheet and two bed-gowns, pawned in the name of Ann Bond, for 1s. 6d.
THOMAS HARLEY. I am servant to Mr. Ross, pawnbroker, of Paddington-street. I have four books called"The Mirror," pawned for 8s. by a young man in the name of George Foster - I cannot say the prisoner is the person.
WILLIAM HENRY BOND. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Osnaburgh-street, Regent's-park. I have a cribbage-board pawned in the evening of the 2d of October, about seven o'clock, in the name of Henry Frost, for 2s. 6d - I cannot identify the prisoner - I do not remember the person.
JAMES HILVER. I live with Mr. Flint, a pawnbroker, of the Edgeware-road. I have a book and three napkins pawned on the 3d of October for 4s. 6d. - and the"Young Man's Companion;" the book, is pawned in the name of Henry Frost, and the napkins in the name of George Warner; they were brought separately, but on the same day - I do not recollect the prisoner's person.(Property produced and sworn to.)
MARY HEWIT. I live at No. 4, Perry-street. I rent the front-room first-floor - my husband is a chair-marker; the prisoner lived in the back-room; when Mrs. Frost came home she complained of being robbed - I had not seen the prisoner in her room - I know nothing more.
EDWARD CONSTANCE. I am the prisoner's brother -(looking at a letter) - I cannot swear to his writing, I have not seen him write for three years; it certainley is like his hand-writing - I believe it to be his; it is signed George Jones and H. Williams - I do not know those persons - (read).
To Mr. Frost, Perry-street, Somer's-town. October 4, 1828.
SIR, - Doubtless ere this you are aware of your loss; it is useless our denying it, therefore we shall make a full confission, and trust to your clemency and kindness. Driven by distress, starvation, and without a place to shelter our heads, when we left Perry-street, we entered your apartment, and took the articles, of which we now return the duplicats, being well aware that they are of greater value than what they are pledged for, and with the intention of making our crime as light a possable; do not emmagin for a moment that we returne them out of bravado - no, rest assured such is not our intentin, but to attone in part for our crimes, and make your loss as little as possable. Sir, I implore, that after this confession you will deal as leniant with us as possable, and not let the law take its course; that we are deserving of yours and the laws correction we well know, but we allso know that you cannot be so unfeeling has to deprive a fellow creature of liberty and reputation, for commiting a crime when urged to it by hungar and distress; under all curcumstances, we we will rely upon your humanity, and throw ourselves entirely upon your mercy -We remain, your verry humble servants, &c, G. JONES and H. WILLIAMS.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. The prisoner was brought to the office on the 29th; I searched him - Frost produced this letter containing six duplicates.
MRS. FROST. I received this letter by the post; it contained six duplicates of the articles produced; the prisoner left the premises on the night of the robbery - I had not heard that he inteuded to go before.
The several pawnbrokers here identified the different duplicates produced.
MARY HEWITT re-examined. My husband rents the house; the prisoner had lived there a fortnight and a fewdays, and left on the night of the robbery - he paid 4s. 6d. a week; he had given no notice at all, nor did he tell us where he was going to - a person named Murray lived in the room with the prisoner, but they came by the name of Jones and Williams, but I did not know which was which; my husband told them if they could not pay they had better leave - I believe that was on the Tuesday, and the robbery was on Thursday; they promised to pay on the Wednesday, but did not.
Prisoner's Defence. The landlord of the house gave us notice to quit if we did not pay our rent the night following; the letters are not my hand-writing.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
But not of breaking and entering.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-241]
200. WILLIAM JONES and JOHN PRICE were indicted for feloniously and burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Carpenter, about two o'clock in the night of the 12th of November, at St. Margaret, Westminster, with intent to steal the goods and chattels therein.
WILLIAM CARPENTER. I keep the Nagg's Head public-house, Tothill-street, in the parish of St. Margaret, Westminster , and rent the whole house; I was the last person up on the night of the 12th of November - I went to bed at eleven o'clock; the doors and windows were all fast; I was alarmed about two o'clock, got up, and some persons were making their escape backwards from the house - I did not see them, but heard them; I could not tell whether it was one person or more; they could get into the Almonry from the back of my house - they had got in through a back door, which I am sure was fastened when I went to bed; I found the bolts were both forced from their places; we found the stock of a centre-bit, a crow-bar, and chisel - they had then broken another, an inner door, which was forced by a crow-bar; the centrebit had not been used to either doors; I had seen the prisoner Jones at my house, about nine o'clock that evening - I knew him by sight; I went to the watch-house, and found the prisoners there about a quarter after two o'clock that night - none of my property was moved.
THOMAS BOYD. I am a watchman; Carpenter's house is on my beat. On the night of the 12th, or early on the morning of the 13th, as I was getting out of my box, a person gave me information, and as I went by the prosecutor's house I stopped a minute at his front door, which leads into Tothill-street, and thought I heard a smothered cough in the house; I went to Cowley, at the next beat, and asked him if the Almonry watchman was on duty; he said he was: I said, "You go to the back-door of the Nag's Head;" he went away - I made no alarm in the front till I thought he had got there; I then made an alarm - the party made their escape, and the two watchmen caught them as they got out; I did not see them till they got to the watch-house, which was about fifty yards off; we found the two doors forced open - this lock was forced off one door, and laid down by the side, and this casement laid on the tap-room table, with the glass broken out.
WILLIAM COWLEY. I am a watchman; my beat joins Boyd's. I received information from Boyd, and went with Brand into the Almonry, where Carpenter has a back door; we went to the door, waited there about half a minute, and the two prisoners came out - they were seized directly; Brand took price, and I took the other - we took them to the watch-hosue; I found the house had been broken open - I did not go into the house; one of the prisoners said, "What are you at?" I said, "You must come with me."
RICHARD BRAND. I am watchman of the Almonry. I received information from Cowley, and went to Carpenter's door - one of the prisoners shoved a bolt back, and as soon as the door was opened, we rushed in and secured them, and took them to the watch-house; we returned to the house, and the housebreaking implements were found just behind the door - I believe three or four doors were broken open; I found a lantern in the Almonry, about five yards from the door.
BENJAMIN TIMBREL. I am constable of the night. The prisoners were brought into the watch-house; I went to Carpenter's house, found a crow-bar, the stock of a centrebit, and a chisel, all in the coal-cellar; Brand delivered me a lantern.
WILLIAM CARPENTER re-examined. I never saw these implements in my house before.
PRICE's Defence. We had been spending the evening with a few friends, and in Tothill-street a female asked us to give her something to drink; we said we would, if she would take us where we could get it - she took us down the Almonry, and said, "If you will give me a shilling, I will fetch the drink;" we staid at the door, waiting for her, when this man came and took us - she is the woman who gave the information, I believe.
JONES. I have no more to say.
JONES - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 19.
PRICE - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 21.
[trialAccount t18281204-242]
245. SARAH ROBINS was indicted for feloniously assaulting Sarah Patterson, and with a certain sharp instrument feloniously, wilfully, and maliciously, did strike, stab, and cut her, in and upon her forehead, with intent to kill and murder her.
TWO OTHER COUNTS, varying the charge.
SARAH PATTERSON. I am the wife of Samuel Patterson, and lodge in Labour-invain-yard, Old Fish-street-hill - my husband is a tailor; I never saw the prisoner before this happened. On the 1st of December, I went into Red Lioncourt, Holywell-lane, to see Mrs. Matthews; it was near five o'clock in the afternoon - I had got very near the door, and there seemed to be a confusion in the court; I saw he prisoner there - she appeared to be in a passion with somebody; as I went up towards the door, the prisoner used some bad expressions - I had not spoken to her; she said, there was a bl-y w-e from Westminster - I never lived at Westminster; I turned round, and asked if she meant to speak of me - she instantly struck me in the mouth, with her fist; I made no answer to her, but immediately went into Mrs. Matthews's house - the street-door was open; she kept continually outside the door, making a noise with other persons, both boys and men; Mrs. Matthews was obliged to keep her door shut for nearly two hours - I was afraid to come out, for she kept saying, if she did not turn me out, (using bad names) she would certainly have mylife, if they staid till morning; I had never seen her before, not to know her - she and others made use of the expressions, and said they would have my life, if they staid till the morning - a little after eight o'clock, my sister came, and knocked at the door repeatedly, and called Mrs. Matthews; at last I knew her voice, and opened the door; instantly the prisoner, with a man named Brown, and several others, rushed in and dragged me out; they got me down, and the man kicked me on the side three times - the prisoner was leaning over me at the time; I saw something in her hand, which looked like the handle of an oyster-knife, but I saw no blade - I instantly felt that I had received a wound on the temple; I cannot swear by whom it was given - my sister and some other persons assisted me up; I received a slight graze on the back of my hand - that was cut very slightly, and also one at the side of my cheek - but it was a severe cut across the temple: the instant the prisoner got up, after I received the wound, she said, "I have done for the bl-y b-h;" I was taken to Dr. Eaton's, and had the wound dressed; he is not here, nor any of his assistants - I lost a great quantity of blood, and have a plaister on now; I have not been confined to my bed, but was very weak, and not able to attend to my business.
Q.Could you at all conjecture the reason of these people having this resentment against you? A. I do not know, unless the prisoner mistook me for somebody else; or she never could have a spite against me; I am very much in dread of my life, for her family are outside here to-day, threatening my life.
Q. Did this appear to you to have been done with a knife, or any hard thing that might have torn the flesh? A. It was done instantly; I cannot say what it was done with.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Have you any recollection of the surgeon saying he thought it must have been done by a blunt instrument? A. No, not exactly; he said it bled profusely, but was not a material hurt, and not dangerous at all; Pritchard is my brother - he was there before I went to Mrs. Matthews'; I found him there - I did not perceive that he was in liquor; I will not swear that he was sober - I swear I was sober myself; I had not been drinking at all, except one glass of gin in the morning; only one glass.
Q. Was there not a general row outside? A. There was nothing but confusion and riot, knocking at Matthews's door with stones, after I got in; there was quarrelling and confusion before I arrived.
COURT. Q.You do not know that it was done with a sharp instrument? A. No. - no medical man is here.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-243]
NEW COURT, Sixth Day.
Fifth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Common Sergeant.
243. MARY PRICE was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of November, 2 half-crowns, and 1 shilling, the monies of John Blackwell, from his person.
JOHN BLACKWELL. I am labourer. I met the prisoner in Whitechapel, between three and four o'clock in the morning of the 9th of November; I turned round the corner of White-Hart court with her, and staid five or ten minutes - I had 12s. in my fob; my trousers were loose -I felt her pushing against me; I took her hand, and found half a crown in it, which she had taken, from my pocket; I then felt my pocket, and there was half a crown more gone - I tried to get it from her; she put it under her clothes - she cried out, and I called the watchman.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you ask what I had in my hand, and I said it belonged to me; you made use of very bad language, and said what I had got you would have; and you thrust your hand into my bosom and under my clothes - I cried Murder! three or four times? A. Yes, she did cry Murder! - I heard a bad sixpence fall on the ground - I did not take her up there; I was standing to get something to drink - I was shut out of my lodging.
JOHN DUNGATE. I am watch-house keeper. The prosecutor said he had been robbed of 12s. - he produced a half-crown and a shilling; I found a half-crown and a bad sixpence secreted on the prisoner - I had before desired her to produce the money, and she said she had none.
JOSEPH BREN. I am a watchman. The prisoner cried Murder! three times, and I came up - she said the man wanted to take liberties with her; he replied that she had robbed him, and he had got half a crown from her - he said she had got half a crown more, and a shilling; he picked up a sixpence from the ground - she said she had not got a halfpenny in the world.
Prisoner's Defence. As I was returning home, down Whitechapel, I met a person I had seen previously, who gave me sixpence to drink his health - it was so bad that no person could offer it - the prosecutor had treated me at a public-house before, and while I was waiting for the person who gave me the sixpence, he came up with two other persons, and said, "Old girl, can you tell me where I can get any thing to drink?" one of them said it was very cold - the prosecutor then said, "Come on, old girl, we are all on the look out;" we went to the side-door of a public-house - the prosecutor stood outside, and the other two called for half a pint of gin, or gin and peppermint; he then said to me, "I want to speak to you;" he went up a private place, and began to take very improper liberties with me - I said, "If you will go to a house I have no objection" - I never touched his pocket; he took nothing from me but the bad sixpence; if it had been a good one he would have sworn it was his - he thrust his hand into my bosom and under my clothes; I called out Murder! he said he would have every farthing of money I had,
JOHN BLACKWELL re-examined. Q.Did you go to any public-house with her? A.There were two young men with her in a public-house: I went in and saw them, took a glass of peppermint, and then went out - I did not know them: we were not together.
JURY to JOHN DUNGATE, Q. Had the prisoner any pockets on? A. No, her pocket was in her stays; I found some duplicates there, but no money - the prosecutor was sober, and accused her of robbing him of 12s., but when they were both searched, the money found corresponded with what he stated.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-244]
214. JEREMIAH SINDERBY was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of December, 12 manger-rings, value 2l. 6s.,the goods of Henry Hall; and that he, at the Delivery of the King's Gaol of Newgate, holden at Justice-hall, in the Old Bailey, on the 11th of September, in the 3d year of his Majesty's reign, was convicted of felony.
HENRY HALL. I am an ironmonger, and live in Red Lion-street. I lost these rings from my counter on the 3d of December; I saw them safe at a quarter before three o'clock, when the prisoner was there; I saw them afterwards taken from the prisoner's pocket.
MATTHEW WELDHEN. I am the street-keeper. The prisoner was given into my custody, and I took these rings from him; he said at first that he did not know any thing about them, but afterwards he said he did it through great distress.
EDWARD HAUGHTON. I am book-keeper to Mr. Hall. The prisoner came in on Wednesday, and asked to look at a lock - while Mr. Hall's back was turned, the prisoner took the rings, and put them into his pocket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went to ask the price of a mortice-lock - I saw a label on these rings; I took them up, and put them down again; I never moved from the place.
WILLIAM READ. I have a certificate of the conviction of Jeremiah Sinderby; I was present at the trial - it was in the Old Court, in 1822; I know the prisoner is the person.(Certificate read.)
GUILTY. Aged 59.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18281204-245]
245. WILLIAM JOHN THOMAS was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, 2 cups, value 2s., and 2 saucers, value 2s., the goods of Edward Foster.
JAMES LOVELL. I was employed by Edward Foster, an auctioneer, of Greek-street, on the 4th of December, at his house, No. 41, Great Coram-street - he had a sale there. I saw the prisoner while the sale was going on - I was watching him for nearly an hour; I did not see him take any thing, but I met him as I was going up stairs - I received information, and followed him; I called to him - he stopped when he had got about forty yards; I went up, and told him I had a suspision he had something on his person - he made some answer, but I cannot exactly tell what; I said "Come, take off your hat, and let me have it - he took off his hat, and gave me one of the cups; I brought him back before Mr. Foster; he said it was a mistake; I was taking him to the watch-house, and he gave me the other cup and saucers out of his pocket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Recommended to Mercy - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18281204-246]
246. MARY CLIFFORD & MARY ANN HASWELL were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of November, 1 box, value 2s.; 3 gowns, value 12s.; 5 pairs of stockings, value 8s.; 2 shifts, value 6s.; 3 petticoats, value 6s.; 3 aprons, value 6d.; 1 shawl, value 2s.; 3 ribbons, value 6d.; 5 caps, value 2s.; 1 brooch, value 10s.; 1 handkerchief, value 2s.; 2 pairs of gloves, value 1s.; and 1 pair of stays, value 6s.; the goods of Elizabeth Massey.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution
ELIZABETH MASSEY. On Saturday, the 15th of November, I came to town from Cambridgeshire to go to service; I had a bundle and a box of my wearing apparel - I lost the direction where I was to go, and left my box and bundle at a public-house - I do not know where; I met Bridget Clifford, in the street - I told her my situation, and she took me to a place in Frying-pan-alley, where I saw the prisoner Clifford, who is her daughter; I stated there that I had my property at a public-house; Haswell came in soon after; she and I went for my things - she stood near the house while I went in and got them, and then we took them to Clifford's house; I slept at Haswell's, which is in the neighbourhood, but my things remained at Clifford's - I had put my things out of the bubdle into the box, and locked it; it was a deal box, with a flat top, about two feet and a half long, and one foot and a half high; I went for it on Monday morning, at eight o'clock, and it was gone; I had seen it there on Sunday, and the two prisoners.
JOHN ABETHAIL. I am an assistant to Mr. Loveday, a pawnbroker, of Gray's Inn-lane. I have a shawl and a cap, pawned on Monday, the 17th of November, by Haswell, in the name of Jones; our house is about three quarters of a mile from Frying-pan-alley.
JAMES PIDDINGTON. I am a watchman. On Sunday night, the 16th of November, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I was in Bagnigge-wells-road - I saw the two prisoners; Clifford had a box on her head; I crossed the road, stopped her, and asked what she had got - Haswell said"It is my brother's box, with his clothes in it;" I then let them pass - it was a deal box, with a flat top, about two feet and a half long, and rather dirty; I knew the prisoners before.
ELIZABETH MASSEY. This is my shawl and cap; I lent the shawl to Clifford, but the cap I had put into the box, about six o'clock on the Sunday evening; I locked it, but the key was in it.
HASWELL'S Defence. She slept with me on the Saturday night, and went to the other house, and had breakfast; I went up again about three o'clock in the afternoon, and they were going to dinner - she said "You are a very good one to keep any one company;" I said there were enough to keep any one company without me; I sat down, and had dinner, and went away; I never saw her again till I was taken.
CLIFFORD'S Defence. I left her at home on the Sunday evening, and she picked up with a gentleman, who gave her a bottle of wine and a supper - I went away - I stopped out all night; the door is not shut - that she knows; she came up in the morning, and I was asleep.
JOHN ROBINSON. I took Clifford into custody, and found this duplicate of the shawl on her.
CLIFFORD - GUILTY. Aged 25.
HASWELL - GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-247]
247. JOHN THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of December, 8 Guernsey jackets, value 12s., the goods of Thomas Taylor.
THOMAS TAYLOR. I am a woollen-draper, and live at Pimlico. I lost eight Guernsey jackets from the door of my shop, on the 3d of December; I had seen them a quarter of an hour before - I heard a cry in the street; I sent my boy out, and in about twenty minutes he brought back the jackets, and the prisoner; I asked how he came to takethem - he made no reply at first, but afterwards said the other gave them to him; there had been another with him, who escaped.
JAMES HAMMOND. I am in the prosecutor's employ. I went into the street. and saw the prisoner and another running - the prisoner had eight Guernsey jackets in his hand- I brought him back with them - it was between eleven and twelve o'clock in the morning; he was about a quarter of a mile from the shop.
DAVID JOHNSON. I got these jackets at Mr. Taylor's - the prisoner was then in custody; Mr. Taylor asked how he came to take them; he said he did not take them; he was coming from Chelsea, and another boy had them; and when he saw the lad follow him, he threw the jackets to him, and asked him to take them - he said he had done nothing, and did not run.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going along Vauxhall-road; I saw a person who asked me to carry this bundle - I put it under my arm; the young man laid hold of my collar, and took me back - the other ran away.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Whipped and Discharged.

248. DANIEL GREEN WHALEY was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of November, - of a yard of kerseymere cloth, value 5s., the goods of Isaac Izard, his master.
ISAAC IZARD. I am a tailor, and live in Gloucester-street, Queen-square. The prisoner was my apprentice, and had been with me about fifteen months; I sent for a constable, on the 3d of November, and asked the prisoner in his presence, to deliver up the key of his box - he, with some reluctance gave it up, when I had threatened to break it open; the constable and I drew it from under the work board, and in it were found this piece of cloth, which I particularly identify as mine.
JOHN MARDON. This is the cloth; I took it out of the box - as we were going to the watch-house, I said it was a great pity to see such a young man taken for robbing his master; he said he was sorry for what was passed, but he could not recall it, and he was ruined.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18281204-249]
249. JOHN SMITH and SARAH SMITH were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, 1 shawl, value 8s., and 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of Margaret Mahoney.
MARGARET MAHONEY. I met the prisoner, Sarah Smith in the City-road, between seven and eight o'clock, on the 4th of December; I knew her before, and went to her room - she asked me to stop to supper; I sat on the bed and she told me to lie my head down if I were sleepy - I I put off my shawl and bonnet and fell asleep; I awoke about three o'clock in the morning, and missed my shawl - I took up my bonnet and asked her for my shawl; she said I did not bring any there; I did not miss my handkerchief from my neck till I got to the watch-house - I did not have any supper; the prisoners were both in the room when I awoke - I was sober when I first met with them but we had some gin; I paid 4 1/2d., and then the man said he would have some more - he had some, and then a pint of porter; that was in a public-house - I am quite sure I had my handkerchief and shawl when I went from the gin-shop to their room.
Prisoner JOHN SMITH. When I came home, she was waiting at the door, and asked me for the key; I gave it to her, and went for a pot of porter, and when I came back, she was in a state of intoxication on the bed - I was not away above ten minutes.
Prisoner SARAH SMITH. Q. Did you not say to me,"Send your children home, and let your * * * go to * *" and say there were to be some races at the Eagle? A. No; I never said any such thing.
WILLIAM HALL. I have the shawl and handkerchief which I found in a dust-hole in the cellar of the house where the prisoners live, they were covered with a saucepan; there are other lodgers in the house; this was on Saturday morning last - I had taken the prisoners on Friday morning; the watchman gave them to me - the prisoners said the prosecutrix did not bring the articles there, and that she was brought there quite drunk by two girls.
WILLIAM FINCH. I am a watchman. I took the prisoners at three o'clock that Friday morning: the prosecutrix cried out Watch! from the one pair of stairs window - she said she had been robbed of her shawl and handkerchief; I went and took the prisoners, but could find nothing.
NOT GUILTY.

250. JOHN ROLF and DANIEL MULLIS were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, 40 yards of canvas cloth, value 15s., the goods of William Bradley.
WILLIAM BRADLEY. I am a green-grocer, and live in Old-street. Mullis has been employed by me occasionally; I saw him last Thursday afternoon near my premises with Rolf, and another person who has been acquitted - they were there three of four hours; I missed the canvas cloth from my back-premises, and suspected Mullis; I went to the officer, and took him to a house in Kingsland-road, kept by Myers - Mullis was with us, and Lewis Myers said in his presence that he bought it of two boys, but Mullis was not one; I had told Mullis if he would tell me where the property was, I would not punish him, and then he told us that he and the others had taken it, and it was at Myers'.
JOHN CLARKE. I am a headborough of St. Luke's. I was present when Mullis was taken; as we were going to the watch-house Mr. Bradley questioned him; he said that he and two more had committed the robbery, and had sold the cloth to Mr. Myers, in Kingsland-road, and he stood outside while the other two went in.
JOHN KNIGHT. I took up Rolf, who said he knew nothing about it.
LEWIS MYERS. I bought the cloth on Thursday evening of Rolf, and another young man; I cannot say whether it was Mullis: I gave 2s. 8d. for it.
ROLF'S Defence. I was coming down the road, and we met two men; one of them had the cloth on his shoulder: he gave us 6d. to take it in there.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoners received a good character, and were recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
ROLF - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Fined One Shilling and Discharged.
MULLIS - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined One Month.

252. JOHN ROBERTSON was again indicted for stealing, on the 6th of May, 4 sovereigns, the monies of Daniel Eden.
DANIEL EDEN. When I was taken from the Cross Keys to the workhouse, on the 6th of May, I asked the prisoner to deposit four sovereigns in Mr. Burroughs' hands, that I might have a few shillings at a time; he said he would, and so help him God! he would come and see me on the Tuesday, but I never saw him any more.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q.Was any one present when you gave him the sovereigns? A. There were two or three people in the room, but they are not here; I was in the hospital two months - when I sent to inquire for the prisoner, they said he was gone to Brighton; I did not know where he was - I went to the Magistrate, and got a warrant; he was taken in Exmouth-street on the 25th of November - I took the officer to inquire about him; I had been twice to his former lodging at Pentonville, and found he had moved to Whitecross-street; I have been in a gentleman's service, but I lost the use of my limbs, and am forced to get my bread as I can- I was in the service of Captain Thorpe, of the Blues, nine years ago, and I have been in business in the public line seven or eight years ago; I got this money by my industry and my friends.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I received the warrant in June or July, but could not find the prisoner till November; I told him I took him for the four sovereigns - he acknowledged in the office having received the money, but he had some little trouble in his house, and he gave 2l. for his wife to get it away.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18281204-253]
253. RICHARD NUTT was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 truck, value 15s., the goods of Samuel Bolton.
SAMUEL BOLTON. I am a coal-dealer, and live in Abbott's-place, Hoxton. The prisoner borrowed a truck of me at a quarter-past two o'clock on the 27th of November - he said he wanted it for Cohen, who lived not one hundred yards off, for two hours.
MANNING ROWLAND. On the 27th of November, about a quarter-past three o'clock, I went to Smithfield, and saw the prisoner dragging a truck; he had just left some person, who had been looking at it: when he came up to me I asked him if he had bought it - he said No, he had got it for sale; I asked what he wanted for it - he said 12s.; I said "It is not just such a thing as suits me" - he said, "I will sell it for 10s.;" I offered him 7s. - he said if I liked it at three half-crowns I should have it; I bought it at last for 7s. - I knew him before.
CHARLES RICKETTS. I took the prisoner in Pitfield-street on the 28th; I saw the truck, and shewed the same to the prosecutor, who accused the prisoner of stealing it, which he denied.
SAMUEL BOLTON re-examined. Q.Did you know him before? A. No; he was with me the first time about two minutes, and then came again and staid about the same time - I am positive he is the person; I met him in the street; I put my hand on his shoulder, and asked how he came to sell my truck in Smithfield; he said,"Don't handle me;" I saw the officer and gave him in charge; I knew Rowland; I met him, and told him I had lost my truck; he said, "I dare say I have bought it."
Prisoner. I made no resistance; I was at home that day from twelve o'clock, and never went out: I know nothing about it.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.

254. WILLIAM MAXFIELD (THE YOUNGER) was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of December, 1 pair of trousers, value 14s., and 1 pair of braces, value 6d., the goods of William Maxfield (the elder.)
WILLIAM MAXFIELD. I am the prisoner's father, I am a shoemaker. On Tuesday, the 2d of December, I sent him into the yard to shake two pieces of carpet, he was gone a considerable time, and as I knew him to be rather bad, I looked out and saw the carpets there; I went down and saw a white cotton stocking in the corner; I then looked into my box, and missed a pair of trousers and a pair of braces which I had put there on the Monday; I went to the pawnbrokers' to tell them to stop them; I searched every where and could not find them; on the Wednesday he sent me the duplicate; these are the trousers.
JOHN GRIFFITHS. I am a pawnbroker. I took in these trousers of the prisoner; he said he pawned them for his father for 9s.
ROBERT TEASDALE. The prisoner's father brought him to me on Wednesday night with this duplicate; I asked him what he had done with the money - he said,"Spent it."

255. CHARLES BAUCES was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of December, 1 hone, value 3s., the goods of William Cadman.
WILLIAM CADMAN. I am a cutler. I lost a new hone on the 3d of December off my counter; I did not see it taken, but my daughter told me it was gone about five o'clock in the afternoon; I had seen it two minutes before: it was found on the prisoner in the shop.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Had not you asked him to strip? A. No; he did appear out of his senses.
ANN CADMAN. I saw the prisoner take the hone, and told my father.
THOMAS AMES. I was sent for, and found the hone in the prisoner's pocket; he appeared tipsy; he had thrown off his jacket, and was throwing it over the counter.
Prisoner' Defence. I had not the least knowledge of it, till it was produced at the Thames Police.
NOT GUILTY.

256. ALEXANDER MORGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, 2 sovereigns, the monies of Alexander Souter.
ALEXANDER SOUTER. I am a sailor. Last Thursday I was in bed at a lodging-house, and about eleven o'clock in the morning I lost two sovereigns from my pocket by my bed-side - I can swear they were in it the night before; there was no other money in it - the prisoner slept in the same room; he was gone as well as the money - I have seen one sovereign and 3s. 2 1/2d. since - the prisoner came to the house before I got up, paid his lodging, and went away; five persons slept in the room - I do not know whether the prisoner had any money the night before.
Prisoner. He had been out all night, and came in the morning very much the worse for liquor. Witness. I been over to the Borough, but came home at six o'clock sober, and went to bed - I had the two sovereigns then; I had my trousers on.
THOMAS PITTLELADDS. I slept in this room - I saw the prosecutor come in; I then turned out, and never went to sleep any mone - I asked the prisoner if he were going to turn out; he said No - I went down, and came in again in a few minutes.
ADAM HIGH. I took the prisoner into custody. On our way to the office my brother officer called out that he had got something in his hand; he shuffled it and tried to put it into his other pocket - I took hold of his hand at the office, and found a sovereign and 3s. 2 1/2d. - he had a new pair of shoes on, hardly soiled, and a second-hand hat, which he said he had bought that morning; he said he had been on board a ship in the London Docks, and had got the money for his work - I said I would go on board the ship; he then said he had it from Berwick, from his wife - I said I knew the Berwick smack, and I would go on board; he then said."No, no, I had it from a man you cannot find" - he was a little in liquor.
ANN PUGH. The prisoner came to our house on the Monday night and slept there; on Tuesday morning he brought his chest to our house, and asked my mistress to lend him 1s. - she said she had no change; he came and asked me to lend him 1s. - I said I had but half a crown, and lent him that; on the Tuesday night he slept at our house - my mistress told me to call him up, and said he should not stop in the house - he asked her to let him stop that night, and said he would go away the next day, which he did.
SAMUEL CROMARTY. I took the prisoner with the other officer; I saw his hand in his pocket; he drew it out, and, I saw a piece of paper, and called to the other officer - I seized his wrist, and he passed the paper out of one hand into the other, and clapped it into his pocket; my brother officer found in it one sovereign and 3s. 2 1/2d.
Prisoner Defence. I went to Blackwall on Tuesday to get a vessel to the West Indies; I went into a public-house to get some bread and beer - a Scotchman there asked me if I knew where he could buy some tools - I got my chest, and sold him my tools for 1l. 15s. 4d.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Confined Six Months.

257. JOHN KING was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of December, 2lbs. weight of butter, value 2s., the goods of Robert Cole.
WILLIAM ALLARD. I am porter to Robert Cole, a cheesemonger, of Wigmore-street. This 2lbs. of butter was taken from my basket, last Wednesday, at a quarter before nine o'clock in the morning; I was in Wyndham-place serving a customer, and I saw the prisoner at the basket -I ran and pursued; he ran into Crawford-street, and he went off, I cried "Stop thief!" and did not lose sight of him but in turning the corner - there was one more person running after him with me. I am sure he is the person I saw at the basket; he had thrown the butter over the rails of an area, which he had passed.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I took the prisoner.
ELIZABETH BURGIN. I saw the boy that was running throw the butter over the rails of the area; I saw two men run after him, and I went over and saw the butter.
Prisoner Defence. I went up the street, and saw the basket; I turned up another street; the man came and said I tried to take something out of his basket - I said I had not.
GUILTY. Aged 17.

258. MARY GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, 1 shovel, value 2s., and 5 pieces of wood, value 2s., the goods of David Sage.
DAVID SAGE. I am a builder. I lost some wood, and a shovel, last Thursday, from a field where I am building; I did not see them taken - but I had been robbed before, and set a man to watch; these are the articles.
THOMAS SAGE. I was desired to watch in the field, where there is a house in an unfinished state; it is called Stone-field, near White Conduit-house. About half-past eight o'clock in the morning, I saw the prisoner come into the field, and go into a cottage; she then came out, and took four pieces of wood - she then took the shovel out of the cottage; she came out with this wood and the shovel; when she got about one hundred yards, I took her with them.
JAMES GRIFFITHS. I am a bricklayer's labourer. This shovel is mine; it was in the house that is building for Mr. Sage.
GEORGE UPWARD. I took the prisoner: she said she hoped Mr. Sage would not be severe with her.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming from work - this young man seized me, and said, "I am looking out for you;" I said, "For what?" he said, "You want to steal that wood over the way:" I said, "No, I don't want to take it."
GUILTY. Aged 38.

259. SARAH GRIFFITH was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of November, 1 bolster, value 6s.; 1 counterpane, value 6s.; 1 looking-glass, value 2s., and 1 flat-iron, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Thomas Riley.
MARY ANN RILEY. I am the wife of Thomas Riley - we live in Devonshire-street, Lisson-grove. The prisoner came to lodge there last Saturday four weeks; on the Monday following, we missed these things - she gave a reference as a servant out of place; I missed a bolster, a counterpane, a looking-glass, and a flat-iron - they were a part of the furniture of the room; she was about leaving, and had come in two or three times in a state of intoxication.
ANN DAVIS. I lodge in this house. The prisoner came there on the Saturday, and slept with me; on the Mondaynight, when I went up to bed, the bolster was gone away; she was lying across the bed, intoxicated - I asked where the bolster was; she said she had pawned it, but her mis - tress, with whom she had lived, in Windham-street, owed her 19s., and she would get it out the next day, before Mr. Riley came home; she was out all the next day - I said to her, in the evening, "Have you got the bolster?" she said No, her mistress had disappointed her; we did not miss the counterpane till Thursday, nor the glass till Friday - she promised to get them every day, but did not.
WALTER FARREN. I am a pawnbroker. I have a bolster pawned on the 10th of November, a looking-glass on the 11th, a counterpane on the 12th, and a flat-iron on the 17th; the looking-glass was pawned by the prisoner.
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON. Webster, who took the prisoner, gave me the duplicates; she said Mrs. Davis gave her leave to pawn them.
MRS. DAVIS. I never gave her leave to pawn them; the duplicates were left in the lodgings.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that the prosecutrix had authorised him to pledge the goods.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined One Month.

260. ELEANOR DWYER was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of October, 7 shillings, the monies of William Harrington.
FRANCES HARRINGTON. I am the wife of William Harrington; he is an umbrella-maker, and keeps a chandler's-shop, in Orris-street, Edgware-road. On the 31st of October, between eleven and twelve o'clock. I saw the prisoner's hand in my till; I was at the door, serving some potatoes - the prisoner had come to see me; she had lived with me three years before: I went into the shop, and she had fled - I pursued, and found her with another girl; she handed something to the other girl: I caught hold of them both, and demanded what she had got; she gave me 3s. 6d.; I found but 6s. 6d. in my till: I had seen 14s. there half an hour before, but I took a good deal while the prisoner was there; the prisoner ran to a hay-lost, and the officer took her there; I had been looking for her an hour or two in the street: she threw me down and struck me; she was a good girl when she lived with me, but she has turned out a horrid bad girl.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18281204-261]
267. EDWARD DEATH was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of November, 1 stove, value 7s. 6d., the goods of John Northfield.
JOHN NORTHFIELD. I am an ironmonger. I lost a stove from my door in the City-road, between two and three o'clock, on the 26th of November; I had seen it two or three hours before: a witness brought the prisoner back with it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
HENRY BLYTH. I took the prisoner with the stove, bout eighty yards from the house; I saw him take it.
JOHN FELLS. I am an officer, and took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I was out looking for a place: I did it for want, and am sorry for it.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18281204-262]
262. JOSEPH CHAPP and JOHN BAKER were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, 1 bottle, value 2s. and 1 quart of Dantzic-spruce, value 2s. the goods of William Greenwood.
WILLIAM GREENWOOD. I am a licensed-victualler, and live in Bagnigge-wells-road. I lost a spruce-beer bottle from my counter last Thursday; I did not see who took it, nor know it was gone, till the officer brought it back, about twelve o'clock; I had seen it in the morning: I noticed the two prisoners come in, and served them with a pint of porter, but I did not see them go out.
JOHN FOSTER. I took this bottle from Baker's pocket, about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's, it was about a quarter-past ten o'clock; he said he had bought it for 1s.: Chapp was with him.
CHAPP'S Defence. I bought it of a brick-maker, (from his appearance,) down by Clerkenwell workhouse.
BAKER'S Defence. I saw Chapp buy it.
CHAPP - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
BAKER - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18281204-263]
260. SARAH CLEMSON was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of November, 1 Bible, value 2s., the goods of John Cunningham.
JOHN CUNNINGHAM. I am a baker, and sleep in a room in Tooley-street. On the 26th of November, the prisoner lodged in the same house; I missed my Bible from my room on the Wednesday at six o'clock in the evening - I had seen it that day.
ALEXANDER RUTTER. I am a constable. On the 1st of December, I was sent for - the landlord told me he had lost a quilt; we found the prisoner - she tore up a duplicate of the Bible, and threw it on the coals.
MICHAEL COMERFORD. I am a pawnbroker. I have a Bible pawned by a woman, not the prisoner, on the 27th of November; I gave a duplicate for it.
RICHARD HARTLEY WALL. I am an officer. There was an application for a search-warrant; I went to the house - the prisoner had been taken by Rutter; we went back, and searched for the duplicate.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I did not pawn the Bible; I had the duplicate in my possession, which, in my flurry I tore, and threw it into the fire-place. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18281204-264]
264. SARAH CLEMSON was again indicted for stealing, on the 1st of December, 1 quilt, value 5s., the goods of John Hine.
ELIZABETH HINE. I am the wife of John Hine; the prisoner lodged at our house. I missed a quilt on the Monday; we sent for an officer - Rutter came, and found the duplicate in the grate - the prisoner said she did not put it there.
ALEXANDER RUTTER. I went on the 1st December, and saw the prisoner - she gave me thirty-seven duplicates out of a tea-caddy; I then saw her hand under her arm, and when I said I must search her; she tore the duplicate in three or four pieces, and threw it under the hearth - this is it.
Prisoner. I did not give you thirty-seven duplicates out of the tea caddy? A. Yes, you did - here they are; you were in liquor.
JAMES WATSON. I took in this quilt, on the 1st of December, of a woman, who gave her name Mary Bennet, and gave this duplicate for it - I do not know the person.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing about it.
GUILTY. Aged 50.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18281204-265]
265. JANE CONNOR was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of November, one clothes-horse, value 3s. the goods of Martha Brown.
MARTHA BROWN. I am a widow and keep a clothes-shop in John-street, Edgware-road; I had a clothes-horse in my passage, on the 28th of November - I had seen it about seven o'clock in the evening, and about half-past seven a witness came and told me of it.
SARAH SPINKS. I saw the prisoner in the passage, near the street-door, with her right elbow resting on the clothes-horse; I was going for a pot of beer - I returned in a few minutes, when the horse and the prisoner were gone.
ANN KEYTON. On the 28th of November two women came to my shop, No. 19, Barrett's-court, and asked me to buy a horse; it might be about eight o'clock in the evening - I bought it for 4s.; I cannot be positive the prisoner was one.
JOHN ARGUST. I took the prisoner, and found the horse at the house for sale; the prisoner always denied having had it.
Prisoner's Defence. I had nothing to do with it - I was washing in the house she lives in at night; I told the person I was washing for I would come in the morning and wash up the place; I went to the door and the witness was going out for beer; she said, "Don't shut the door till I come in;" I said, "I will not;" I put on my pattens and went home; I went the next morning and the woman sent for the officer to take me.
NOT GUILTY.
[supplementaryMaterial o18281204-1]
The KING v. WILLIAM VYSE, convicted at the September Session.
The Learned Judges having confirmed the conviction in this case, the prisoner received sentence of Transportation for Life.
